Global Regular Solutions of the Multidimensional Degenerate Compressible Navier-Stokes Equations with Large Initial Data of Spherical Symmetry
Gui-Qiang G. Chen, Jiawen Zhang, Shengguo Zhu

Abstract
. A fundamental open problem in the theory of the compressible Navier-Stokes equations is whether regular spherically symmetric flows can develop singularities – such as cavitation or implosion – in finite time. A formidable challenge lies in how the well-known coordinate singularity at the origin can be overcome to control the lower or upper bound of the density. For the barotropic Navier-Stokes system with constant viscosity coefficients, recent striking results have shown that such implosions do indeed occur. In this paper, we show that the situation is fundamentally different when the viscosity coefficients are degenerately density-dependent (as in the shallow water equations). We prove that, for general large spherically symmetric initial data with bounded positive density, solutions remain globally regular and cannot undergo cavitation or implosion in two and three spatial dimensions. Our results hold for all adiabatic exponents γ ∈ ( 1 , ∞ ) \gamma\in(1,\infty) in two dimensions, and for physical adiabatic exponents γ ∈ ( 1 , 3 ) \gamma\in(1,3) in three dimensions, without any restriction on the size of the initial data. To achieve these results, we make carefully designed weighted radial estimates via a region segmentation method, which is the key for obtaining uniform control over both the density and the effective velocity. The methodology developed here should also be useful for solving other related nonlinear partial differential equations involving similar difficulties.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35A01, 35Q30, 76N10, 35B65, 35A09.

1.
Introduction

The global regularity of solutions of the multidimensional (M-D) compressible Navier-Stokes equations (
CNS
) with large initial data is a longstanding open problem, even when the initial data exhibit some form of symmetry. The main obstacle lies in obtaining uniform
a priori
bounds on the density, both above and below, as has been extensively discussed by Lions
[
58
]
, Hoff-Smoller
[
40
]
, Feireisl
[
25
]
, Huang-Matsumura
[
44
]
, among many others. Recently, for the three-dimensional (3-D) barotropic
CNS
with constant viscosity coefficients, it has been shown by Merle, Raphaël, Rodnianski, and Szeftel
[
62
]
and by Buckmaster, Cao-Labora, and Gómez-Serrano
[
10
]
that there exist smooth, spherically symmetric initial data for which the corresponding solutions undergo finite-time implosion at the origin, with the density blowing up to infinity. In stark contrast, in this paper, we establish the well-posedness of global regular solutions for general smooth, spherically symmetric initial data with positive and bounded density for the following
CNS
with degenerately density-dependent viscosity coefficients (as in the shallow water equations)
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(
n=2
or
3
):

\begin{cases}\rho_{t}+\diver(\rho\boldsymbol{u})=0,\\[3.0pt]
(\rho\boldsymbol{u})_{t}+\diver(\rho\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u})+\nabla P=2\alpha\diver(\rho D(\boldsymbol{u})),\end{cases}

(1.1)

with the initial data:

(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(0,\boldsymbol{x})=(\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})=(\rho_{0}(|\boldsymbol{x}|),u_{0}(|\boldsymbol{x}|)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{|\boldsymbol{x}|})\qquad\text{for $\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$},

(1.2)

and the far-field asymptotic condition:

\displaystyle(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})\to(\bar{\rho},\boldsymbol{0})\qquad\text{as $|\boldsymbol{x}|\to\infty\,$ for $\,t\geq 0$},

(1.3)

where
t\geq 0
and
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\cdots\!,x_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
denote the time and Eulerian spatial coordinates, respectively,
\rho\geq 0
is the density of the fluid and the initial density

\rho_{0}

here is positive and bounded,

\boldsymbol{u}=(u_{1},\cdots\!,u_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
stands for the velocity of fluid,

P
is the pressure,

D(\boldsymbol{u})=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{u})^{\top})
is the strain tensor, and
\alpha>0
and
\bar{\rho}\geq 0
are both constants. For the polytropic gas, the constitutive relation is given by

P=A\rho^{\gamma},

where
A>0
is the entropy constant and
\gamma>1
is the adiabatic exponent.

When
\gamma=n=2
, (
1.1
) corresponds to the viscous shallow water equations,

\begin{cases}h_{t}+\diver(h\boldsymbol{u})=0,\\[3.0pt]
(h\boldsymbol{u})_{t}+\diver(h\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u})+A\nabla(h^{2})={V}(h,\boldsymbol{u}),\end{cases}

(1.4)

where
h
is the height of fluid surface,
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the horizontal
velocity, and
{V}(h,\boldsymbol{u})
is the viscosity term. For the spherically symmetric flow, since
D(\boldsymbol{u})=\nabla\boldsymbol{u}
,
then in (
1.4
),

{V}(h,\boldsymbol{u})=2\alpha\diver(hD(\boldsymbol{u}))=2\alpha\diver(h\nabla\boldsymbol{u}).

(1.5)

We refer to
[
2
,
29
,
30
,
58
,
59
,
68
]
and and the references therein for more details on the viscous shallow water system.

Recently, the two-dimensional (2-D) shallow water equations have attracted considerable attention.
Nevertheless, as Lions already emphasized in his 1998 monograph
[
58
]
,
the global existence of multidimensional solutions of the Cauchy
problem of system (
1.4
)–(
1.5
) with general initial data remains
completely open, especially in the context of regular solutions.
A notable milestone was reached by Vasseur-Yu
[
79
]
in 2016,
who proved the global existence of multidimensional weak solutions with vacuum.
Their result provided the first rigorous global existence result for weak solutions,
yet left unanswered a further fundamental question of whether regular solutions
persist globally.
In this paper, we solve this longstanding open problem by establishing,
for the first time, the global existence of large-data regular solutions.
The results established here are not only fundamental in the mathematical theory
of
CNS
,
but also extend naturally to physically relevant models such as
in the shallow water theory.

In stark contrast with the recent results of
[
10
,
62
]

for
CNS
with constant viscosity coefficients,
we prove that, for general smooth spherically symmetric
data in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(
n=2
,
3
),
the regular solutions to the degenerate
CNS
(
1.1
)
cannot develop cavitation or implosion in a finite time,
provided the initial density is positive and bounded.
The central innovation here lies in developing carefully designed weighted radial estimates via a region segmentation method,
to overcome the well-known obstacle of the coordinate singularity
at the origin. This framework yields uniform control of both the lower
and upper bounds of the density, thereby establishing global regularity of solutions.

There is a vast literature on the well-posedness of solutions to
CNS

when the viscosity coefficients are constants and the initial density is strictly positive. The one-dimensional (1-D) problem has been studied extensively;
see
[
14
,
40
,
48
,
51
,
52
,
72
,
87
]
and the references therein.
In the M-D case, the local well-posedness of classical solutions of the Cauchy problem
follows from the standard symmetric hyperbolic-parabolic structure satisfying
the well-known Kawashima’s
condition; see
[
45
,
50
,
65
,
73
,
76
]
and the references therein.
Matsumura-Nishida
[
60
,
61
]
first established the global existence of 3-D classical solutions for initial data close to a non-vacuum equilibrium in the Hilbert space
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
We also refer the reader to Danchin
[
20
,
21
]
for
global strong solutions with small initial data in some Besov spaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}

for
n\geq 2
, and Hoff
[
37
,
38
]
for global weak solutions with small discontinuous data in some Sobolev spaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n=2
,
3
.
For M-D spherically symmetric flow, Jiang
[
46
]
established the global existence of smooth solutions with
large data in the domain exterior to a ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n=2
or
3
, and
Hoff
[
36
]
proved the global existence of weak solutions of the Cauchy problem of isothermal flow in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n\geq 2

when the initial data are large and discontinuous.
It is worth pointing out that the analysis in
[
36
]
allows the possibility that a vacuum state emerges at the origin.
Some other related progress can also be found
in
[
16
,
39
]
and the reference therein.

In general, a vacuum is required for the far-field
under some physical requirements
such as finite total mass and total energy in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. However, the approaches used in the references mentioned above do not work directly
for the case when cavitation appears, owing to the degeneracy of the time evolution
in the momentum equations, which makes it difficult to study the dynamics of the fluid velocity near the vacuum.
In fact, in the view of physics, it is not clear how the fluid velocity can be defined when there is no fluid.
On the other hand, in terms of mathematical structures, the time evolution equations of the fluid velocity in
CNS
with constant viscosity coefficients,
i.e.
the momentum equations,
is a parabolic system in the fluid region,
but degenerates to an elliptic one near the vacuum region.
By introducing some initial compatibility conditions, the local well-posedness of 3-D regular solutions with vacuum was established successfully
in Salvi-Straškraba
[
71
]
and Cho-Choe-Kim
[
17
]
.
Later, Huang-Li-Xin
[
42
]
proved the global well-posedness of classical solutions
with small data for the barotropic
CNS
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

(see also Choe-Kim
[
18
]
for the global spherically symmetric, smooth solutions with
large data in annular domains).
The main breakthrough for the well-posedness of M-D solutions with generic data
is due to Lions
[
58
]
, where the global existence of weak solutions with finite energy for the barotropic
CNS
was established
when the pressure
P
satisfies
P=A\rho^{\gamma}
with
\gamma\geq\frac{9}{5}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

and
\gamma\geq\frac{3}{2}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
For the global existence of weak solutions with finite energy in M-D barotropic flow,
we also refer the reader to Feireisl-Novotný-Petzeltová
[
27
]

for
\gamma>\frac{3}{2}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\gamma>1
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
Bresch-Jabin
[
7
]
for the thermodynamically unstable pressure and anisotropic viscous stress tensor, and
Jiang-Zhang
[
47
]
for the spherically symmetric, weak solutions
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n=2,3
when
\gamma>1
. However, the uniqueness problem of these M-D weak solutions to
CNS
obtained in
[
7
,
27
,
47
,
58
]

is widely open due to their fairly low regularity.

It is worth pointing out that, when the viscosity coefficients
are constants, some singular behaviors of solutions with vacuum to
CNS

have been observed. Recently, studies in
[
23
,
84
]

show that the classical solutions with vacuum of the Cauchy problem
of the M-D
CNS
cannot preserve the conservation of momentum,
no matter whether
\rho_{0}
is compactly supported or just vanishes in the far-field.
On the other hand, Hoff-Serre
[
41
]
showed that, when vacuum appears,
the weak solutions of the 1-D barotropic
CNS
need not continuously depend
on their initial data.
In particular, these counterintuitive behaviors can be attributed
to the unphysical assumption
that the viscosity coefficients are constants
when one utilizes such a kind of
CNS
to deal with the vacuum problems
in viscous fluids, which makes the vacuum exert a force on the fluid at the interface that
separates the vacuum and the fluid,
according to the classical impulse-momentum theorem (
cf
.
[
32
]
).
Thus, viscous compressible fluids near the vacuum should be better modeled by the degenerate
CNS
, which can be derived from the Boltzmann equations through the Chapman-Enskog expansion; see Chapman-Cowling
[
13
]
.

In fact, the degenerate
CNS
for the barotropic flow has received extensive
attention in recent years. Many key progresses have been made
on the global well-posedness of smooth
solutions when the initial density is strictly positive;
see
[
19
,
35
,
49
,
64
]
for 1-D flow with large data,
and
[
75
,
80
]
for 2-D flow with initial data close
to a non-vacuum equilibrium.
However, when
\inf_{\boldsymbol{x}}\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})=0
,
the momentum equations are degenerate both in the
time evolution and spatial dissipation, which makes it formidable to establish the propagation and mollification mechanisms of the regularity of solutions.
In the M-D case, a remarkable framework was initiated with a series of papers
by Bresch-Desjardins
[
4
,
5
]
(started in 2003 with Lin
[
6
]

in the context of Navier-Stokes-Korteweg with a linear shear viscosity coefficient case),
which provides additional information related to the gradient of a function of

\rho

when the viscosity coefficients satisfy what is called the Bresch-Desjardin constraint.
This information is now called the BD entropy, which
plays an important role in the development of the global existence of
M-D weak solutions with finite energy of the degenerate
CNS
;
see Mellet-Vasseur
[
63
]
for the compactness theory, and Bresch-Vasseur-Yu
[
8
]
, Li-Xin
[
54
]
, and
Vasseur-Yu
[
79
]
for the existence theory.
Recently, by some elaborate analysis of the intrinsic degenerate-singular
structures of the degenerate
CNS
,
the local well-posedness of regular solutions with far-field vacuum has been established
in
[
55
,
56
,
84
]
.
In the domain exterior to a ball, Cao-Li-Zhu
[
11
]
proved the global existence
of
3-D
spherically
symmetric regular solutions with large data and far-field vacuum
with the help of the BD entropy estimate. Moreover, the global well-posedness of regular solutions with vacuum in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
for a class of initial data with small density (but possibly large velocity)
was established by Xin-Zhu
[
83
]
.
Some other related progress can also be found
in
[
31
,
34
,
85
,
86
]
and the reference therein.

Despite these significant progresses mentioned above for
CNS
,
no matter with or without any symmetry assumption,
the global regularity of large solutions in
\mathbb{R}^{n}

(n\geq 2)
remains an open problem, which is extremely difficult due to the possible cavitation and implosion inside the fluids. As far as we know, there is no solid progress for compressible viscous flow along this direction in a positive way until now, except for some interesting attempts to the barotropic
CNS
with artificial viscosities; see

[
43
,
78
]
and the references therein. On the other hand, several negative results in this direction have been obtained. First, when the viscosity coefficients are constants, it is shown in Xin
[
82
]
that when the initial density is compactly supported, any smooth solutions in
H^{s}
(for suitably large
s
) of the Cauchy problem of the M-D non-isentropic
CNS
without heat conduction will blow up in finite time, which has been extended to the case that the initial density vanishes only at far-fields with a fast decay rate by Rozanova
[
69
]
. However, it remains unclear whether the solutions considered in
[
69
,
82
]
exist locally in time in the M-D case.
Recently, for the 3-D spherically symmetric flow, Merle-Raphaël-Rodnianski-Szeftel
[
62
]
prove that there exists a set of finite-energy smooth initial data with far-field vacuum for which the corresponding solutions to the barotropic
CNS
implode (with infinite density) in finite time, which has been extended to the case that the initial density is strictly positive by Buckmaster, Cao-Labora, and Gómez-Serrano
[
10
]
. Later, for the 3-D barotropic
CNS
without any symmetry assumption, Cao-Labora, Gómez-Serrano, Shi, and Staffilani
[
12
]
constructed some smooth solutions that are strictly away from the vacuum and develop an imploding finite time singularity in
\mathbb{T}^{3}
(torus) or
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. For the barotropic
CNS
with degenerate viscosity coefficients, it is shown in
[
56
,
83
,
86
]
that, for certain classes of 3-D initial data with vacuum in some open set, one can construct the corresponding local classical solutions in inhomogeneous Sobolev space, which will break down in finite time, no matter how small and smooth the initial data are.

In this paper, we establish the global well-posedness of regular solutions of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) for general smooth initial data of spherical symmetry
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n=2,3
, which is the first global existence theorem for regular solutions
for this problem for the M-D compressible viscous flow. In fact, for the spherically symmetric flow with the radial coordinate
variable
r=|\boldsymbol{x}|
, system (
1.1
) can be reformulated into

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\rho_{t}+(\rho u)_{r}+\underbrace{\frac{m\rho u}{r}}_{\star}=0,\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle\underbrace{(\rho u)_{t}+(\rho u^{2})_{r}}_{\circledast}+P_{r}-\underbrace{2\alpha(\rho u_{r})_{r}}_{\Diamond}-\underbrace{2m\Big(\alpha\Big(\frac{\rho u}{r}\Big)_{r}+\frac{\alpha\rho_{r}u}{r}+\frac{\rho u^{2}}{2r}\Big)}_{\star}=0,\end{cases}

(1.6)

where
\star
denotes the coordinates singularity,
\circledast
denotes the degenerate
time evolution, and
\Diamond
denotes the degenerate spatial dissipation.

Some favorable regularity properties may be anticipated, since (
1.6
) exhibits
largely one-dimensional behavior away from the origin.
Nevertheless, due to the compressibility of fluids,
our analysis encounters two major obstacles:

•

possible cavitation,
i.e.

\rho(t,r)\to 0
for some
(t,r)\in(0,T]\times[0,\infty)
;

•

possible implosion,
i.e.

\rho(t,r)\to\infty
for some
(t,r)\in(0,T]\times[0,\infty)
.

Overcoming these difficulties is particularly challenging because of several inherent issues:

•

the coordinate singularity at the origin, manifested by the singular factor
\tfrac{1}{r}
in system (
1.6
);

•

the degeneracies in both the time evolution (
\circledast
) and the spatial dissipation (
\Diamond
), arising either from the far-field vacuum in the case
\bar{\rho}=0
in (
1.3
), or from the possible occurrence of cavitation.

In fact, almost all known results on the global spherically symmetric strong solutions with large
data (see
[
11
,
18
,
46
,
81
]
and the references therein) are established
on the domains that exclude the origin.
Moreover, for the barotropic
CNS
with constant viscosity coefficients,
when the domain does include the origin, some crucial observations have been made regarding
the occurrence of implosion and cavitation at the origin:

•

For certain classes of spherically symmetric initial data, it was shown in
[
10
,
62
]

that the corresponding local regular solutions develop an implosion in finite time
and that such singularities indeed form at the origin.
More precisely, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\quad\ \ \ \ \lim_{t\to T_{*}}\rho(t,0)=\infty,\quad\lim_{t\to T_{*}}\sup_{r\in[0,\,\varepsilon)}|\boldsymbol{u}(t,r)|=\infty\qquad\text{for some }T_{*}\in(0,\infty).

(1.7)

•

In Devault-Grémaud-Jenssen
[
22
]
, the numerical results indicate that there exist weak solutions to
CNS
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(
n=2
or
3
) which display the formation of cavitation
at the origin when the initial data are discontinuous and sufficiently large. In this case, the initial density is constant, while the initial velocity field is symmetric, points radially outward, and belongs to
H^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}
for all
s<\frac{n}{2}
.

These observations suggest that it is unlikely one could establish the global existence of regular spherically symmetric solutions with large data for the multidimensional barotropic
CNS
on domains containing the origin,
regardless of whether the initial density is strictly positive or not.

Fortunately, by exploiting the intrinsic degenerate-singular structure of (
1.1
)
in radial coordinates,
e.g.
(
1.6
),
together with a careful analysis, we establish the global well-posedness
of regular spherically symmetric solutions of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
)
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n=2,3
, provided the initial density is positive and bounded
in the whole space.
This holds for general smooth spherically symmetric initial data, without restriction on their size.
In particular, our results indicate that
the large solution of
CNS
in two and three dimensions never develops
cavitation or implosion in finite time, as long as the initial density is positive and bounded.
To achieve this, the central difficulty lies in establishing a uniform upper bound for
\rho
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
which is highly intricate due to the obstacles discussed above.
Our main contribution is to develop new radial weighted estimates for both the density
\rho
and the effective velocity

\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{u}+2\alpha\nabla\log\rho
via
a region segmentation method.
This framework allows us, for the first time, to simultaneously control the density and effective velocity across the entire domain, including the delicate singular region near the origin.
More precisely, in the exterior region, the well-known BD entropy estimate yields the desired upper bound for
\rho
, while, in the unit ball, we employ our new radial weighted estimates to control both
\rho
and
\boldsymbol{v}
. A further key observation is that, for spherically symmetric flows, the radial projection of the effective velocity
\boldsymbol{v}
satisfies a damped transport equation. Combining this structure with the upper bound of
\rho
and the method of characteristics allows us to deduce the boundedness of
\boldsymbol{v}
. Finally, once the boundedness of
(\rho,\boldsymbol{v})
is secured, the energy estimates yield the corresponding lower bound for
\rho
, thereby ensuring global regularity.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows:
In §
2
, we present the main theorems of the paper.
In §
3
, we outline the main strategies underlying the proof of global well-posedness as stated in §
2
.
In §
4
–§
9
, we provide a detailed proof of the global well-posedness for regular solutions with far-field vacuum of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
)
with general smooth spherically symmetric initial data.
In particular, in §
4
–§
8
,
we establish the global uniform estimates for the regular solutions in carefully designed function spaces. This is achieved in the following four steps:

(i)

Derive the global-in-time
a priori
upper bound for
\rho
(§
4
);

(ii)

Establish the global uniform
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
-estimate for the effective velocity (§
5
);

(iii)

Prove that cavitation at the origin cannot occur in finite time (§
6
);

(iv)

Establish global uniform estimates for
2
- and
3
-order regular solutions (§
7
–§
8
).

Based on these steps, in §
9
, we obtain the global well-posedness
of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) for regular solutions with far-field vacuum,
by using the method of continuity.
Furthermore, in view of the double-degenerate structure of (
1.6
)
induced by the far-field vacuum, the local well-posedness of regular solutions with far-field vacuum
(which is crucial for the arguments in §
4
–§
9
) is highly nontrivial. This is established in §
11
.

On the other hand, in §
10
,
we address the case when the initial density is strictly positive
and establish the global well-posedness of regular solutions for general smooth,
spherically symmetric data. In fact, we show that, with suitable modifications,
the methodology developed in §
4
–§
9

for treating the large-data problems with far-field vacuum can also be successfully applied to the corresponding large-data problems with strictly positive initial density. Finally, we list some auxiliary lemmas and new Sobolev embedding theorems
for spherically symmetric functions that are used frequently throughout this paper
in Appendices
A
–
C
.

2.
Main Theorems

This section is devoted to stating our main theorems on the global well-posedness
of regular solutions of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
)
with large initial data of spherical symmetry in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n=2,3
.
For simplicity, throughout this paper,
for any function space defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the following conventions are used for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
unless otherwise specified:

\begin{split}&\|f\|_{L^{p}}=\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\quad\|f\|_{H^{k}}=\|f\|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\quad\|f\|_{W^{k,p}}=\|f\|_{W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\\[4.0pt]
&D^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\big\{f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\,\|f\|_{D^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|\nabla^{k}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\infty\big\},\\[4.0pt]
&D^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=D^{k,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\|f\|_{D^{k,p}}=\|f\|_{D^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\quad\|f\|_{D^{k}}=\|f\|_{D^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\\[4.0pt]
&H^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ W^{0,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=D^{0,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ H^{-k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\big(H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\big)^{*}.\end{split}

(2.1)

2.1.
Global spherically symmetric solutions of the degenerate
CNS
with far-field vacuum

We first address the case that
\bar{\rho}=0
in (
1.3
).

We consider the following physical range of the adiabatic exponent
\gamma
in system (
1.1
):

\gamma\in(1,\infty)\ \ \text{if}\ \ n=2,\qquad\quad\gamma\in(1,3)\ \ \text{if}\ \ n=3.

(2.2)

Notice that, if
\rho>0
, the momentum equations
\eqref{eq:1.1benwen}_{2}
can be formally rewritten as

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}+\frac{A\gamma}{\gamma-1}\nabla\rho^{\gamma-1}+L\boldsymbol{u}=\nabla\log\rho\cdot Q(\boldsymbol{u}),\end{split}

(2.3)

where
L\boldsymbol{u}
and
Q(\boldsymbol{u})
are given by

L\boldsymbol{u}=-\alpha\Delta\boldsymbol{u}-\alpha\nabla\diver\boldsymbol{u},\qquad Q(\boldsymbol{u})=2\alpha D(\boldsymbol{u}).

(2.4)

If (
2.3
) is used to study the time evolution of
\boldsymbol{u}
,
then it can transfer the degeneracies both in the time evolution and spatial dissipation
to the possible singularity of
\nabla\log\rho
. Therefore, the two quantities

(\rho^{\gamma-1},\nabla\log\rho)

will play significant roles in our analysis on the high-order regularity of the fluid velocity

\boldsymbol{u}
.
Due to this observation, for the case that
\bar{\rho}=0
in (
1.3
),
we first introduce a proper class of solutions, called regular solutions, of
the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
).

Definition 2.1
.

Assume that

\bar{\rho}=0
in (
1.3
),
s=2
or
3
, and
T>0
.
The vector function
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
is called an
s
-order regular solution of
the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)
–
(
1.3
) in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

(n=2
or
3)
,
if

\begin{split}\mathrm{(i)}&\ (\rho,\boldsymbol{u})\ \text{satisfies this problem in the sense of distributions};\\
\mathrm{(ii)}&\ 0<\rho\in C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad\nabla\log\rho\in L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}),\\[2.0pt]
&\ \nabla\rho^{\gamma-1}\in C([0,T];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad(\rho^{\gamma-1})_{t}\in C([0,T];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\\[2.0pt]
&\ \nabla^{2}\log\rho\in C([0,T];H^{s-2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad(\nabla\log\rho)_{t}\in C([0,T];H^{s-2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}));\\[2.0pt]
\mathrm{(iii)}&\ \boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\\[2.0pt]
&\ \boldsymbol{u}_{t}\in C([0,T];H^{s-2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})).\end{split}

Remark 2.1
.

We first introduce some physical quantities to be used in this paper
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho(t,\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\qquad(\textrm{total mass}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho(t,\boldsymbol{x})\boldsymbol{u}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\qquad(\textrm{momentum}).

It will be shown later that the
s
-order regular solutions defined here satisfy the conservation of
\mathcal{M}(t)
and
\mathcal{P}(t)
. Next, the regularity of
\rho
shown in
Definition
2.1

implies that
\rho>0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
but decays to zero in the far-field,
which is consistent with the facts that
\mathcal{M}(t)
and
\mathcal{P}(t)
are both conserved,
and
CNS
is a model of non-dilute fluids. Thus, the definition of the s-order regular solutions are consistent with the physical background of
CNS
.

Our first theorem is on the global well-posedness of the
2
-order regular solutions
of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) with large initial data of spherical symmetry.
The regularity of this solution provides the uniqueness
in both the 2-D and the 3-D cases. However, the
2
-order regular solution is a classical one in the 2-D case, but not in the 3-D case.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
n=2
or
3
,
\bar{\rho}=0
in (
1.3
), and (
2.2
) hold.
Assume that the initial data
(\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})
are spherically symmetric
and satisfy

0<\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ \ \nabla\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1}(\boldsymbol{x})\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ \ \nabla\log\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ \ \boldsymbol{u}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\\

(2.5)

and, in addition,

\nabla\log\rho_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\quad\,\text{ when }\ n=3.

(2.6)

Then, for any
T>0
, the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)
–
(
1.3
) admits a unique global
2
-order regular solution
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})

in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
that satisfies

\begin{split}&\sqrt{t}\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];D^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad\sqrt{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\\
&\sqrt{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})).\end{split}

(2.7)

Moreover,
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
is spherically symmetric with the form
:

(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})=(\rho(t,r),\,u(t,r)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r})\qquad\,\,\mbox{for $r=|\boldsymbol{x}|$},

(2.8)

and satisfies the following properties
:

\mathrm{(i)}

When
n=2
,
the solution we obtain here is classical
:

\displaystyle(\rho,\nabla\rho,\boldsymbol{u})\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad(\rho_{t},\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u}_{t})\in C((0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2});

(2.9)

\mathrm{(ii)}

When
n=3
, the solution we obtain here is classical
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{*}:=\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\,|\boldsymbol{x}|=0\}

except the origin
:

\displaystyle(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad(\nabla\rho,\rho_{t})\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{*}),

(2.10)

\displaystyle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{*})\cap C((0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad(\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u}_{t})\in C((0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{*});

\mathrm{(iii)}

(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
satisfies
the conservations of total mass and total momentum
(
that remains zero
):

\mathcal{M}(t)=\mathcal{M}(0),\quad\,\mathcal{P}(t)\equiv\boldsymbol{0}\qquad\ \text{for $t\in[0,T]$};

(2.11)

\mathrm{(iv)}

For any
T>0
and
(t,r)\in(0,T]\times[0,\infty)
,

\begin{gathered}\min\big\{C(T)^{-1},\,(e^{-1}\underline{\rho}(r))^{C(T)(\sqrt{r}+1)}\big\}\leq\rho(t,r)\leq\min\big\{C(T),Cr^{-n+1}\big\},\end{gathered}

(2.12)

where
\underline{\rho}(r):=\inf_{z\in[0,r]}\rho_{0}(z)
,
C\geq 1
is a constant depending only on
(\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
and
(n,\alpha,\gamma,A)
, and
C(T)\geq 1
is a constant depending only on
(C,T)
.

Our second theorem is on the global well-posedness of the
3
-order regular solutions of
the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) with large initial data of spherical
symmetry.
The regularity of this solution not only provides the uniqueness in both the 2-D and 3-D cases,
but also shows that it is a classical solution of this problem considered.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
n=2
or
3
,
\bar{\rho}=0
in (
1.3
), and (
2.2
) hold.
Assume that the initial data
(\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})
are spherically symmetric
and satisfy

\begin{split}&0<\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\nabla\rho_{0}^{\gamma-1}(\boldsymbol{x})\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\\
&\nabla\log\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).\end{split}

(2.13)

Then, for any
T>0
, the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)
–
(
1.3
) admits a unique global
3
-order regular solution
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})

in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
that satisfies

\begin{split}&\sqrt{t}\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];D^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad\sqrt{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\\
&\sqrt{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})).\end{split}

(2.14)

Moreover,
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
is spherically symmetric and satisfies (
2.8
), (
2.11
)
–
(
2.12
), and

\mathrm{(i)}

When
n=2
, the solution we obtain here
is a classical solution
:

\displaystyle(\rho,\nabla\rho,\rho_{t},\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\boldsymbol{u},\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u}_{t})\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2});

(2.15)

\mathrm{(ii)}

When
n=3
, the solution we obtain here
is a classical solution
:

\displaystyle(\rho,\nabla\rho,\rho_{t},\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\boldsymbol{u})\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad(\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u}_{t})\in C((0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}).

(2.16)

In addition, when
\gamma\geq\frac{3}{2}
, the regularity assumptions
imposed on
\rho^{\gamma-1}_{0}
in (
2.5
) and (
2.13
) can be removed.

Corollary 2.1
.

For
\gamma\in\big[\frac{3}{2},\infty\big)
when
n=2
and
\gamma\in\big[\frac{3}{2},3\big)

when
n=3
, the initial conditions (
2.5
)
–
(
2.6
) in
Theorem
2.1
can be reduced to

0<\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\nabla\log\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\\

(2.17)

and, in addition,

\nabla\log\rho_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\qquad\text{ when $n=3$};

(2.18)

and the initial condition (
2.13
) in
Theorem
2.2
can be reduced to

0<\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\nabla\log\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(2.19)

2.2.
Global spherically symmetric solutions of the degenerate
CNS
with strictly positive initial density

Next we consider the case that
\bar{\rho}>0
in (
1.3
). For simplicity, when the initial density is strictly positive, we define the corresponding regular solutions of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
).

Definition 2.2
.

Assume that
\bar{\rho}>0
in (
1.3
),
s=2
or
3
, and
T>0
.
The vector function
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
is called an
s
-order regular solution of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)
–
(
1.3
) in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

(n=2
or
3)
, if

\begin{split}\mathrm{(i)}&\ (\rho,\boldsymbol{u})\ \text{satisfies this problem in the sense of distributions};\\
\mathrm{(ii)}&\ \inf_{(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho(t,\boldsymbol{x})>0,\quad\rho-\bar{\rho}\in C([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\\[2.0pt]
&\,\,\nabla\rho\in L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\rho_{t}\in C([0,T];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}));\\[2.0pt]
\mathrm{(iii)}&\ \boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\\[2.0pt]
&\ \boldsymbol{u}_{t}\in C([0,T];H^{s-2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})).\end{split}

When the initial density is strictly positive, our main results on the global well-posedness
of the
s
-order regular solutions of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) with
large initial data of spherical symmetry
(s=2,3)
are stated in the following two theorems:

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
n=2
or
3
,
\bar{\rho}>0
in (
1.3
), and (
2.2
) hold.
Assume that the initial data
(\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})
are spherically symmetric
and satisfy

\inf_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})>0,\quad(\rho_{0}-\bar{\rho},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

(2.20)

and, in addition,

\qquad\nabla\rho_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\ \text{ when $n=3$}.

(2.21)

Then, for any
T>0
, the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)
–
(
1.3
)
admits a unique global
2
-order regular solution
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})

in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Moreover,
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
is spherically symmetric with form (
2.8
)
and satisfies (
2.7
)
–
(
2.11
) and, for any
T>0
,

C(T)^{-1}\leq\rho(t,\boldsymbol{x})\leq C(T)\qquad\,\,\text{for $\,(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}$},

(2.22)

where
C(T)\geq 1
is a constant depending only on
(T,\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\bar{\rho},n,\alpha,\gamma,A)
.

Similar to the flow with far-field vacuum,
the
2
-order regular solution with strictly positive initial density is a classical solution when
n=2
, but not when
n=3
. In order to obtain the corresponding classical solutions for
n=3
, we need to establish the following global well-posedness of the
3
-order regular solutions.

Theorem 2.4
.

Assume that
n=2
or
3
,
\bar{\rho}>0
in (
1.3
), and (
2.2
) holds.
If the initial data
(\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})
are spherically symmetric
and satisfy

\inf_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})>0,\quad(\rho_{0}-\bar{\rho},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

(2.23)

then, for any
T>0
, the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)
–
(
1.3
)
admits a unique global
3
-order regular solution
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})

in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Moreover,
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
is spherically symmetric with form (
2.8
)
and satisfies (
2.11
), (
2.14
)
–
(
2.16
),
and (
2.22
).

2.3.
Applications to the 2-D shallow water equations

Another purpose of this paper is to establish the well-posedness of regular solutions
with large data applicable to physically relevant models in the shallow water theory. In fact, for the spherically symmetric flow, when
{V}=2\alpha\diver(hD(\boldsymbol{u}))
or
2\alpha\diver(h\nabla\boldsymbol{u})
in (
1.4
),
due to

D(\boldsymbol{u})=\nabla\boldsymbol{u}
,
(
1.4
) is a special case of
system (
1.1
) with
\gamma=n=2
.
Therefore, we can simply replaces
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u},\bar{\rho})
by
(h,\boldsymbol{u},\bar{h})

in Theorems
2.1
–
2.4
to obtain the same conclusion for these 2-D viscous
shallow water models.
More precisely, we consider the Cauchy problem of (
1.4
) with the initial data:

(h,\boldsymbol{u})(0,\boldsymbol{x})=(h_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})=(h_{0}(|\boldsymbol{x}|),u_{0}(|\boldsymbol{x}|)\dfrac{\boldsymbol{x}}{|\boldsymbol{x}|})\qquad\text{for $\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$},

(2.24)

and the far-field asymptotic condition:

\left(h,\boldsymbol{u}\right)(t,\boldsymbol{x})\to(\bar{h},\boldsymbol{0})\qquad\,\,\text{as $\,\left|\boldsymbol{x}\right|\to\infty\,$ for $t\geq 0$},

(2.25)

where
\bar{h}\geq 0
is a fixed constant.
We establish the global spherically symmetric (classical) solutions taking the form:

(h,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})=(h(t,|\boldsymbol{x}|),u(t,|\boldsymbol{x}|)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{|\boldsymbol{x}|}).

(2.26)

First, when
\bar{h}=0
, the regular solutions of the Cauchy problem (
1.4
) with (
2.24
)–(
2.25
) can be defined analogously to Definition
2.1

with
\gamma=n=2
, and
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
replaced by
(h,\boldsymbol{u})
.
Then, from Theorems
2.1
–
2.2
and Corollary
2.1
,
the following conclusions hold:

Corollary 2.2
.

Let
{V}=2\alpha\diver(hD(\boldsymbol{u}))
or
2\alpha\diver(h\nabla\boldsymbol{u})
,
and
\bar{h}=0
. If the initial data
(h_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})
are
spherically symmetric and satisfy

0<h_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\nabla\log h_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

then, for any
T>0
, the Cauchy problem (
1.4
) with (
2.24
)
–
(
2.25
) admits a unique
2
-regular solution
(h,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})

in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
that satisfies (
2.7
). Moreover,
(h,\boldsymbol{u})
is spherically symmetric with form (
2.26
)
and satisfies (
2.9
) and (
2.11
)
–
(
2.12
)
with
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
replaced by
(h,\boldsymbol{u})
.

Corollary 2.3
.

Let
{V}=2\alpha\diver(hD(\boldsymbol{u}))
or
2\alpha\diver(h\nabla\boldsymbol{u})
, and
\bar{h}=0
. If the initial data
(h_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})
are spherically symmetric, and satisfy

0<h_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\nabla\log h_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

then, for any
T>0
, the Cauchy problem (
1.4
)
with (
2.24
)
–
(
2.25
) admits a unique
3
-regular
solution
(h,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})

in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
that satisfies (
2.14
). Moreover,
(h,\boldsymbol{u})
is spherically symmetric with form (
2.26
) and
satisfies (
2.11
)
–
(
2.12
) and (
2.15
)
with
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
replaced by
(h,\boldsymbol{u})
.

Second, when
\bar{h}>0
, the regular solutions of the Cauchy problem (
1.4
) with (
2.24
)–(
2.25
) can be defined analogously to Definition
2.2
, with
\gamma=n=2
and
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u},\bar{\rho})
replaced by
(h,\boldsymbol{u},\bar{h})
.
Then, from Theorems
2.3
–
2.4
, the following conclusions hold:

Corollary 2.4
.

Let
{V}=2\alpha\diver(hD(\boldsymbol{u}))
or
2\alpha\diver(h\nabla\boldsymbol{u})
, and
\bar{h}>0
. If the initial data
(h_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})
are spherically symmetric and satisfy

\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}h_{0}(x)>0,\qquad(h_{0}-\bar{h},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

then, for any
T>0
, the Cauchy problem (
1.4
)
with (
2.24
)
–
(
2.25
)
admits a unique
2
-regular solution
(h,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})

in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
that satisfies (
2.7
).
Moreover,
(h,\boldsymbol{u})
is spherically symmetric with form (
2.26
)
and satisfies (
2.9
), (
2.11
),
and (
2.22
) with
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u},\bar{\rho})

replaced by
(h,\boldsymbol{u},\bar{h})
.

Corollary 2.5
.

Let
{V}=2\alpha\diver(hD(\boldsymbol{u}))
or
2\alpha\diver(h\nabla\boldsymbol{u})
, and
\bar{h}>0
. If the initial data
(h_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})
are spherically symmetric
and satisfy

\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}h_{0}(x)>0,\qquad(h_{0}-\bar{h},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

then, for any
T>0
, the Cauchy problem (
1.4
)
with (
2.24
)
–
(
2.25
) admits
a unique
3
-regular solution
(h,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})

in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}

).
Moreover,
(h,\boldsymbol{u})

)
and satisfies (

), (

),
and (

)
with

replaced by

.

We now make some remarks on the results of this paper.

Remark 2.2
.

In the global-in-time well-posedness results
obtained in
Theorems
2.1
–
2.4
,
there are no restrictions on the size of the initial data.
On the other hand, when the initial data allow a far-field vacuum,
the initial condition (
2.5
)
–
(
2.6
) or (
2.13
) identifies
a class of spherically symmetric initial data that provides the unique solvability
of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)
–
(
1.3
).
For example, one can choose
(\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
satisfying the following constraints
:

\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}u_{0}(r)

with
u_{0}(r)\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}((0,\infty))
,
and
0<\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

with
\rho_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})=\rho_{0}(r)
such that

\mathrm{(i)}

\rho_{0}(r)\rightarrow 0
algebraically as
r\rightarrow\infty
:

0<\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{\iota}\rho_{0}(r)<\infty\qquad\text{for $\,\iota>\max\big\{n,\frac{n}{2\gamma-2}\big\}$};

\mathrm{(ii)}

\rho_{0}(r)\rightarrow 0
exponentially as
r\rightarrow\infty
:

0<\lim_{r\to\infty}e^{r^{\iota}}\rho_{0}(r)<\infty\qquad\text{for $\,0<\iota<2-\frac{n}{2}$};

\mathrm{(iii)}

\rho_{0}(r)\rightarrow 0
as
r\rightarrow\infty
,
and the decay rate is faster than that of the exponential rate
:

0<\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{r^{\iota}}\rho_{0}(r)<\infty\qquad\text{for $\,0<\iota<2-\frac{n}{2}$}.

Remark 2.3
.

We give some comments on the constraints for
\rho_{0}
given
in
Theorems
2.1
–
2.4
.
The boundedness of the effective velocity

\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{u}+2\alpha\nabla\log\rho
plays a key role in our analysis,
which requires that
\boldsymbol{v}(0,\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Since
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
holds by classical Sobolev embedding theorems,

\nabla\log\rho_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is required.

In
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, by the following critical Sobolev embedding for spherically symmetric
vector functions
(
see
Lemma
C.4

in
Appendix
C
):

D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

(\nabla\log\rho_{0},\,\nabla\rho_{0})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

are implied by
\nabla\log\rho_{0}\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
in (
2.5
) and (
2.13
),
and
\nabla\rho_{0}\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
in (
2.20
) and (
2.23
),
respectively.

In
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, by the following Sobolev embedding for spherically symmetric vector functions
(
see
Lemma
C.2
):

D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\hookrightarrow L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

and the embedding
L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\,\cap D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\hookrightarrow\,L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
due to the classical Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality,
(\nabla\log\rho_{0},\nabla\rho_{0})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
are implied by
(\nabla\log\rho_{0},\nabla\rho_{0})\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
in (
2.13
) and (
2.23
), respectively.
However, when
n=3
in
Theorems
2.1
and
2.3
, the assumptions that
\nabla\log\rho_{0}\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\rho_{0}-\bar{\rho}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
do not yield
(\nabla\log\rho_{0},\nabla\rho_{0})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
which is the reason why this condition is imposed additionally
when
n=3
in (
2.6
) and (
2.21
).

Remark 2.4
.

For the
2
-
D
case,
Theorems
2.1
–
2.4
hold for any
\gamma\in(1,\infty)
, while these theorems are valid for
\gamma\in(1,3)
for the
3
-
D
case.

This distinction arises primarily from the factor
r^{\frac{m}{2}}

(m=n-1)
in the BD entropy estimates under the spherical coordinates
(
see
Lemma
4.1
in
§
4
and
Lemma
10.2
in
§
10
)
:

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big\|r^{\frac{m}{2}}(\sqrt{\rho})_{r}\big\|_{L^{2}(0,\infty)}\leq C_{0},

(2.27)

where
C_{0}>0
is a constant depending only on
(\rho_{0},u_{0},\alpha,\gamma,A,n)
and
\bar{\rho}
if
\bar{\rho}>0
. Note that (
2.27
) can be treated as some weighted
L^{2}(0,\infty)
-estimates of
(\sqrt{\rho})_{r}
with the different weight function
r^{\frac{m}{2}}
in
(0,\infty)
.
In fact, in
§
4
and
§
10
, for establishing the global uniform upper bound for
\rho
on
[0,T]\times[0,1)
, the main challenging issue
is the well-known coordinate singularity at the origin. A key idea to overcome the difficulty is to employ the Hardy
inequality
(
see
Lemma
A.3
in
Appendix
A
)
and the BD entropy estimate (
2.27
) to derive some new
r
-weighted estimates for
\rho
on
[0,T]\times[0,1)
:

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|r^{K}\rho\|_{L^{p}(0,1)}\leq C\qquad\,\text{for $p\in[1,\infty]$ and some proper $K=K(p)$}.

(2.28)

However, due to the dimension-dependent weight function
r^{\frac{m}{2}}
in (
2.27
),
the exponent
K
in (
2.28
) differs between the
2
-
D
and the
3
-
D
cases
:

for the
2
-
D
case,
K>-\frac{1}{p}
, while
K\geq 1-\frac{1}{p}
for the
3
-
D
case.
Since the range of
K
is smaller in the
3
-
D
case,
it narrows the admissible range of
\gamma
in subsequent calculations.
Through an elaborate analysis based on (
2.28
) in
§
4

and
§
10
, we are able to establish the global upper bound
of
\rho
on
[0,T]\times[0,1)
for all
\gamma\in(1,\infty)
in the
2
-
D
case,
and for all
\gamma\in(1,3)
in the
3
-
D
case as shown in
Theorems
2.1
–
2.4
.
More detailed calculations can be found in
§
3.3.2
.

Remark 2.5
.

The global well-posedness results obtained in
Theorems
2.1
–
2.2
for the flow with far-field vacuum
and
Theorems
2.3
–
2.4
for the flow with strictly positive
initial density indicate that the uniform upper and lower bounds of density
\rho

at the origin are not necessarily related to the far-field behavior of the solutions.

Remark 2.6
.

Under proper modifications, when the initial density is positive and bounded,
the methodology developed in this paper can be applied to proving the global well-posedness
of regular solutions with general smooth, spherically symmetric initial data
of the corresponding Cauchy problem of the barotropic
CNS

with nonlinear density-dependent viscosity coefficients
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n=2
,
3
, which will be addressed
in our forthcoming paper
[
15
]
.

3.
Notations, Reformulations, and Main Strategies

In this section, we first present some notations and conventions in §
3.1
, which will be frequently used throughout this paper. Next, in §
3.2
, we introduce an enlarged reformulation for the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) to deal with the degeneracy caused by the far-field vacuum, which makes the corresponding problem trackable. In §
3.3
, based on such a reformulation, we show the main strategy and new ideas in our analysis for the flow with far-field vacuum. Finally, in §
3.4
, we show that, under proper modifications, the methodology developed here for dealing with the large-data problems
with far-field vacuum can also be applied to solving the corresponding large-data problems with strictly
positive initial density.

3.1.
Notations

The notations and conventions in this paper are given as follows:

3.1.1.
Notations in M-D Eulerian coordinates

Throughout the rest of this paper,
unless otherwise specified, we adopt the notations in (
2.1
) and the following ones:

•

We always let
n=2
or
3
be the dimension of the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and denote
m:=n-1
.

•

For a variable
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
(
l\geq 2
), its
i
-th component is denoted by
y_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq l
), and
\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{1},\cdots\!,y_{l})^{\top}
. We always let
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\cdots\!,x_{n})^{\top}
be the spatial variable of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

For any vector function
\boldsymbol{f}:E\subset\mathbb{R}^{l}\to\mathbb{R}^{q}
(
l,q\geq 2
,
E
is a measurable set), its
i
-th component is denoted by
f_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq q
), and
\boldsymbol{f}=(f_{1},\cdots\!,f_{q})^{\top}
.

•

For any function
f
defined on a measurable subset of
\mathbb{R}^{l}
(
l\geq 1
), if the independent variable of
f
is
\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{1},\cdots\!,y_{l})^{\top}
, then

\displaystyle\qquad\partial_{y}^{\varsigma}f=\partial_{y_{1}}^{\varsigma_{1}}\cdots\partial_{y_{l}}^{\varsigma_{l}}f=f_{\underbrace{\text{\tiny$y_{1}\cdots y_{1}$}}_{\text{$\varsigma_{1}$-times}}\cdots\underbrace{\text{\tiny$y_{l}\cdots y_{l}$}}_{\text{$\varsigma_{l}$-times}}}=\frac{\partial^{\varsigma_{1}+\cdots+\varsigma_{l}}}{\partial y_{1}^{\varsigma_{1}}\cdots\partial y_{l}^{\varsigma_{l}}}f\quad\ \text{for }\varsigma=(\varsigma_{1},\cdots\!,\varsigma_{l})\in\mathbb{N}^{l},

\displaystyle\qquad\nabla_{y}f=(\partial_{y_{1}}f,\cdots\!,\partial_{y_{l}}f)^{\top},\qquad\Delta_{y}f=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\partial_{y_{i}}^{2}f,

\displaystyle\qquad\nabla_{y}^{k}f\text{ denotes one generic }\partial_{y}^{\varsigma}f\text{ with }|\varsigma|=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\varsigma_{i}=k\text{ for integer }k\geq 2,

\displaystyle\qquad|\nabla_{y}^{k}f|=\Big(\sum_{|\varsigma|=k}|\partial_{y_{1}}^{\varsigma_{1}}\cdots\partial^{\varsigma_{l}}_{y_{l}}f|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\text{ for }k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}.

In particular, for the derivatives with respect to the
variable
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\cdots\!,x_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
we use the notations

(\partial_{i}^{\varsigma_{i}},\partial^{\varsigma},\nabla,\Delta,\nabla^{k})=(\partial_{x_{i}}^{\varsigma_{i}},\partial_{x}^{\varsigma},\nabla_{x},\Delta_{x},\nabla_{x}^{k})
.

•

If
\boldsymbol{f}:E\subset\mathbb{R}^{l}\to\mathbb{R}^{q}
(
l,q\geq 2
,
E
is a measurable set) is a vector function with the independent variable
\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{1},\cdots\!,y_{l})^{\top}
and
X\in\{\partial_{y_{i}}^{\varsigma_{i}},\partial_{y}^{\varsigma},\Delta_{y},\nabla_{y}^{k}\}
, then

\displaystyle X\boldsymbol{f}=\big(Xf_{1},\cdots\!,Xf_{q}\big)^{\top},\ \quad\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{f}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{y_{1}}f_{1}&\partial_{y_{2}}f_{1}&\cdots&\partial_{y_{l}}f_{1}\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{y_{1}}f_{2}&\partial_{y_{2}}f_{2}&\cdots&\partial_{y_{l}}f_{2}\\[5.69054pt]
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{y_{1}}f_{q}&\partial_{y_{2}}f_{q}&\cdots&\partial_{y_{l}}f_{q}\end{pmatrix}_{q\times l},

\displaystyle|\nabla_{y}^{k}\boldsymbol{f}|=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{|\varsigma|=k}\big|\partial_{y_{1}}^{\varsigma_{1}}\cdots\partial^{\varsigma_{l}}_{y_{l}}f_{i}\big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\text{ for }k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}.

Moreover, if
l=j+i
with
j\geq 0
and the independent variable
\boldsymbol{y}
takes the form
\boldsymbol{y}=(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{\tilde{y}})^{\top}
with
\boldsymbol{s}=(s_{1},\cdots\!,s_{j})^{\top}
and
\boldsymbol{\tilde{y}}=(\tilde{y}_{1},\cdots\!,\tilde{y}_{i})^{\top}
, then

\diver_{\tilde{y}}\boldsymbol{f}=\sum_{k=1}^{i}\partial_{\tilde{y}_{k}}f_{k}.

In particular, if
j=0,1
,
\,i=n
, and
\boldsymbol{\tilde{y}}=\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\cdots\!,x_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then
\diver=\diver_{x}
.

3.1.2.
Notations in M-D spherical coordinates

In the rest of this paper, unless otherwise specified, we always let
r=|\boldsymbol{x}|
be the radial distance in spherical coordinates, and let
r\in I:=[0,\infty)
. Moreover, the following conventions are adapted:

\displaystyle|f|_{p}=\|f\|_{L^{p}(I)},\quad\|f\|_{k}=\|f\|_{H^{k}(I)},\quad\|f\|_{k,p}=\|f\|_{W^{k,p}(I)}\qquad\,\,\mbox{for $k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}$}.

In particular, for the Sobolev spaces defined on the open interval
(a,b)\subset I
, we use the abbreviation
X(a,b)=X((a,b))
for
X=L^{p}
,
W^{k,p}
, and
H^{k}
.

3.1.3.
Other notations and conventions

•

C^{\ell}(\overline{\Omega})

(\ell\in\mathbb{N},\,C(\overline{\Omega})=C^{0}(\overline{\Omega}))

denotes the space of all functions
f
for which
\nabla^{j}f

(0\leq j\leq\ell)
is bounded and uniformly continuous in
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which is equipped with the norm:

\|f\|_{C^{\ell}(\overline{\Omega})}:=\max_{0\leq j\leq\ell}\|\nabla^{j}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}.

In particular, if
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
C^{\ell}(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
denotes the space of all functions
f
for which
\nabla^{j}f

(0\leq j\leq\ell)
is bounded and uniformly continuous in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

C^{\infty}_{\rm c}(\Omega)
denotes the space of all functions
f
for which
\nabla^{j}f

(j\in\mathbb{N})
is continuous and compactly supported in
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

For any function spaces
(X,X_{1},\cdots\!,X_{k})
and functions
(h,g,g_{1},\cdots\!,g_{k})
,

\|g\|_{X_{1}\cap\cdots\cap X_{k}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|g\|_{X_{i}},\qquad\|h(g_{1},\cdots\!,g_{k})\|_{X}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|hg_{i}\|_{X}.

•

For any
n\times n
real matrix
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{A}_{ij}
denotes its
(i,j)
-th entry.
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{B}:=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{ij}\mathcal{B}_{ij}
for any
n\times n
matrices
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
. Moreover,
\mathrm{SO}(n)
denotes the set of all
n\times n
real orthogonal matrices
\mathcal{O}
such that
\det\mathcal{O}=1
, where
\det\mathcal{O}
is the determinant of
\mathcal{O}
.

•

\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker symbol satisfying
\delta_{ij}=1
when
i=j
,
and
\delta_{ij}=0
otherwise.

•

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{X^{*}\times X}
denotes the pairing between the space
X
and its dual space
X^{*}
.

3.2.
An enlarged reformulation

In order to deal with the degeneracy caused by the far-field vacuum, we now introduce an enlarged reformulation
for the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
).
Specifically, by introducing the following variables:

\phi=\frac{A\gamma}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma-1},\quad\boldsymbol{\psi}=\frac{1}{\gamma-1}\nabla\log\phi=\nabla\log\rho=(\psi_{1},\cdots\!,\psi_{n})^{\top},

(3.1)

then
CNS
(
1.1
) can be rewritten as the following enlarged system:

\begin{cases}\phi_{t}+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\phi+(\gamma-1)\phi\diver\boldsymbol{u}=0,\\[2.0pt]
\boldsymbol{u}_{t}+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}+\nabla\phi+L\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{\psi}\cdot Q(\boldsymbol{u}),\\[2.0pt]
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\psi}_{t}+\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi})+\nabla\diver\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{0},\end{cases}

(3.2)

where the operators
L
and
Q
are defined in (
2.4
).

We aim to establish global spherically symmetric solutions of (
3.2
) with the form:

(\phi,\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\psi})(t,\boldsymbol{x})=(\phi(t,|\boldsymbol{x}|),\,u(t,|\boldsymbol{x}|)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{|\boldsymbol{x}|},\,\psi(t,|\boldsymbol{x}|)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{|\boldsymbol{x}|}),

(3.3)

with the initial data:

\displaystyle(\phi,\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\psi})(0,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=(\phi_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})

(3.4)

\displaystyle:=(\phi_{0}(|\boldsymbol{x}|),\,u_{0}(|\boldsymbol{x}|)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{|\boldsymbol{x}|},\,\frac{1}{\gamma-1}(\log\phi_{0})_{r}(|\boldsymbol{x}|)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{|\boldsymbol{x}|})\qquad\text{for $\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$},

and the far-field asymptotic condition:

\begin{split}\displaystyle\left(\phi,\boldsymbol{u}\right)(t,\boldsymbol{x})\to\left(0,\boldsymbol{0}\right)\qquad\text{as $\left|\boldsymbol{x}\right|\to\infty\,$ for $\,t\geq 0$}.\end{split}

(3.5)

Then we define the corresponding regular solutions of
problem (
3.2
)–(
3.5
).

Definition 3.1
.

Let
s=2
or
3
, and
T>0
. The vector function
(\phi,\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\psi})

is called a
s
-order regular solution of problem (
3.2
)
–
(
3.5
)
in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

(n=2
or
3)
if

\begin{split}\mathrm{(i)}&\ (\phi,\boldsymbol{u})\ \text{satisfies this problem in the sense of distributions};\\
\mathrm{(ii)}&\ 0<\phi^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}\in C([0,T];L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\ \ \boldsymbol{\psi}\in L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ \ \nabla\phi\in C([0,T];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\\
&\,\,\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C([0,T];H^{s-2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\ \ \phi_{t}\in C([0,T];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\ \ \boldsymbol{\psi}_{t}\in C([0,T];H^{s-2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}));\\
\mathrm{(iii)}&\ \boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\\
&\ \boldsymbol{u}_{t}\in C([0,T];H^{s-2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T];D^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})).\end{split}

Since
\partial_{i}\psi_{j}=\partial_{j}\psi_{i}
(
i,j=1,\cdots\!,n
),
then equations
\eqref{eqn1}_{3}
can be rewritten as

\boldsymbol{\psi}_{t}+\sum\limits_{l=1}^{n}A_{l}(\boldsymbol{u})\partial_{l}\boldsymbol{\psi}+B(\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{\psi}+\nabla\diver\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{0},

(3.6)

where
A_{l}(\boldsymbol{u})=(a_{ij}^{(l)})_{n\times n}\,(i,j,l=1,\cdots\!,n)
are symmetric with
a_{ij}^{(l)}=u_{l}
when
i=j
and
a_{ij}^{(l)}=0
otherwise,
and
B(\boldsymbol{u})=(\nabla\boldsymbol{u})^{\top}
.
Then the enlarged system (
3.2
) consists of

•

a
scalar transport
equation
\eqref{eqn1}_{1}
for
\phi
;

•

a
parabolic
system
\eqref{eqn1}_{2}
with the weak singular source term
\boldsymbol{\psi}\cdot Q(\boldsymbol{u})
for
\boldsymbol{u}
;

•

a
symmetric hyperbolic
system
\eqref{eqn1}_{3}
with the source term
\nabla\diver\boldsymbol{u}
for
\boldsymbol{\psi}
.

The enlarged reformulation in (
3.2
) transfers the degeneracies both in the time evolution and spatial dissipation to the singularity of
\boldsymbol{\psi}
, which enables us to establish the following local well-posedness of regular solutions of problem (
3.2
)–(
3.5
) in Theorem
3.1
–
3.2
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
n=2
or
3
, and
\gamma
satisfy

\gamma>1.

(3.7)

Assume that the initial data
(\phi_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})

are spherically symmetric and satisfy

\begin{gathered}0<\phi_{0}^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}(\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ \ \nabla\phi_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ \ \boldsymbol{\psi}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ \ \boldsymbol{u}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\end{gathered}

(3.8)

and, in addition,

\begin{gathered}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\qquad\text{when $n=3$}.\end{gathered}

(3.9)

Then there exist
T_{*}>0
such that problem (
3.2
)
–
(
3.5
) admits a unique
2
-order regular solution
(\phi,\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\psi})(t,\boldsymbol{x})
in
[0,T_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying (
2.7
) with
T
replaced by
T_{*}
, and

\boldsymbol{\psi}(t,\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{\gamma-1}\nabla\log\phi(t,\boldsymbol{x})\qquad\text{for {\it a.e.} $(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in(0,T_{*})\times\mathbb{R}^{n}$}.

(3.10)

Moreover,
(\phi,\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\psi})
is spherically symmetric with form (
3.3
).

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
n=2
or
3
, and (
3.7
) hold. Assume that
the initial data
(\phi_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})
are spherically symmetric
and satisfy

\displaystyle 0<\phi_{0}^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}(\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\nabla\phi_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

(3.11)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\boldsymbol{\psi}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Then there exist
T_{*}>0
such that problem (
3.2
)
–
(
3.5
) admits a unique
3
-order regular solution
(\phi,\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\psi})(t,\boldsymbol{x})
in
[0,T_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying (
2.14
) with
T
replaced by
T_{*}
, (
3.10
), and

\phi_{tt}\in C([0,T_{*}];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T_{*}];D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad\boldsymbol{\psi}_{tt}\in L^{2}([0,T_{*}];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})).

(3.12)

Moreover,
(\phi,\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\psi})
is spherically symmetric with form (
3.3
).

The proofs for Theorems
3.1
–
3.2
will be given in §
11
.
Moreover, at the end of §
11
,
we will show that Theorems
3.1
–
3.2
indeed imply the local well-posedness theories
of regular solutions of problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
)
with general smooth, spherically symmetric data
and far-field vacuum (
i.e.
,
\bar{\rho}=0
),
which are stated in Theorems
3.3
–
3.4
below.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
n=2
or
3
, and (
3.7
) hold.
If the initial data
(\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})
are spherically symmetric and satisfy (
2.5
)
–
(
2.6
), then there exists
T_{*}>0
such that problem (
1.1
)
–
(
1.3
) admits a unique
2
-order regular solution
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})
in
[0,T_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying (
2.7
)
–
(
2.10
) with
T
replaced by
T_{*}
. Moreover,
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
is spherically symmetric with form (
2.8
).

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
n=2
or
3
, and (
3.7
) hold. If the initial data
(\rho_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})(\boldsymbol{x})
are spherically symmetric, and satisfy (
2.13
), then there exists
T_{*}>0
such that
problem (
1.1
)
–
(
1.3
) admits a unique
3
-order regular solution
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})(t,\boldsymbol{x})
in
[0,T_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying (
2.14
)
–
(
2.16
) with
T
replaced by
T_{*}
, and

(\rho^{\gamma-1})_{tt}\in C([0,T_{*}];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\cap L^{2}([0,T_{*}];D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad(\nabla\log\rho)_{tt}\in L^{2}([0,T_{*}];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})).

Moreover,
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u})
is spherically symmetric with form (
2.8
).

Furthermore, we point out that this enlarged reformulation in (
3.2
) plays an important role
in the global-in-time energy estimates for our solutions (see §
7
–§
8
).

3.3.
Main strategies for the case that
\bar{\rho}=0

Now we briefly outline our main strategies to establish Theorems 3.1–3.4 when
\bar{\rho}=0
. To overcome the difficulties caused by the degeneracy in the far-field
and the coordinate singularity at the origin, the main ingredients of our analysis consist of

§
3.3.1
:

establishing some new critical Sobolev embedding theorems for spherically symmetric functions (see Appendix
C
), which do not hold in the general case;

§
3.3.2
:

establishing some new
r
-weighted
L^{p}(0,1)
-estimates of
\rho

for
p\in[1,\infty]
, and
L^{p}(I)
-estimates of the effective velocity for some
p\in(n,\infty)

in deriving the global uniform upper bound of
\rho

in
[0,T]\times I
(see §
4.2
);

§
3.3.3
:

developing an unconventional technique based on the
L^{p}(I)
-estimates
of
\rho^{\frac{1}{p}}u
for
p\in[2,\infty)
for establishing the global
L^{\infty}(I)
-estimate
of the effective velocity (see §
5
);

§
3.3.4
:

proving that cavitation does not form in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
for any finite time
T
inside the fluids and establishing some
pointwise lower bound estimate of
\rho
(see §
6
);

§
3.3.5
:

establishing the global uniform estimates for
the regular solutions (see §
7
–§
8
).

Throughout the rest of §
3.3
,
C_{0}\in[1,\infty)
denotes a generic constant depending only on
(\rho_{0},u_{0},n,\alpha,A,\gamma)
,
and
C(\nu_{1},\cdots\!,\nu_{k})\in[1,\infty)
denotes a generic constant depending on
C_{0}
and parameters
(\nu_{1},\cdots\!,\nu_{k})
, which may be different at each occurrence.

3.3.1.
Local well-posedness and some new critical Sobolev embeddings

To establish the desired local well-posedness theory, we first study the linearized
problem (
11.1
) with spherical symmetry initial data and then obtain the local
well-posedness of (
3.2
)–(
3.5
) via the classical Picard iteration.
To maximize the range of the admissible initial density,
in the well-posedness theory for
2
-order regular solutions stated in Theorem
3.1
,
we merely assume that
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{0}\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
if
n=2

and
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
if
n=3
, without requiring any
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
-integrability on itself,
which makes the corresponding local well-posedness highly non-trivial.
To overcome it, we establish some novel critical Sobolev embeddings
for spherically symmetric functions in Appendix
C
.

We first outline the idea for the 2-D case. Under the spherical symmetry assumption, we prove that,
if a vector function
\boldsymbol{f}\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
then
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and

\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C_{0}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

(3.13)

which can be found in Lemma
C.4
in Appendix
C
.
This shows that the Sobolev embeddings
D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

hold for all spherically symmetric vector fields, which do not hold in the general case.
Hence, by the symmetric hyperbolic structure of the linearized system for
\boldsymbol{\psi}

(
i.e.
,
\eqref{li4}_{3}
),
we obtain the
D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-estimate for
\boldsymbol{\psi}
which, along with its spherical symmetry and (
3.13
), yields the
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-estimate of
\boldsymbol{\psi}
.
Fortunately, such estimates on
\boldsymbol{\psi}
enable us to establish the local well-posedness to (
3.2
)–(
3.5
). Furthermore, in the 2-D case, with the help of these Sobolev embeddings
in Lemmas
C.4
–
C.6
,
we show that the
2
-order regular solutions are actually classical ones
of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) (see §
9.1
).

For the 3-D case, the major difficulty lies in establishing the
a priori

L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-estimate of
\boldsymbol{\psi}
for the linear problem (
11.1
).
Formally, this requires the
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-boundedness of
\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{w}

which, however, is not contained in the regularity assumptions of
\boldsymbol{w}
in (
11.4
).
To solve this issue, under the spherical symmetry assumption,
we prove that, if a vector function
\boldsymbol{f}\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, then
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and

\|\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{f}}\|_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C_{0}\|\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{f}}\|_{D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})},

(3.14)

which can be found in Lemma
C.2
in Appendix
C
. Unlike the classical case in which (
3.14
) holds for the functions in
L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

(see Lemma
A.1
(i) in Appendix
A
),
we can directly obtain the
L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-boundedness of
\boldsymbol{f}

from (
3.14
) with
\boldsymbol{f}\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
solely due to its spherical symmetry.
Hence, we consider the linearized problem (
11.1
) equipped with the initial data
(\phi_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{0})
satisfying the regularity assumption (
11.2
),
with
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{0}\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
only.
By the symmetric hyperbolic structure of the linearized system
for
\boldsymbol{\psi}
(
i.e.
,
\eqref{li4}_{3}
),
we obtain the
D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-estimate for
\boldsymbol{\psi}
which,
along with its spherical symmetry and (
3.14
), yields the
L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-estimate
of
\boldsymbol{\psi}
.
Fortunately, such estimates on
\boldsymbol{\psi}
enable us to establish the local well-posedness
to (
3.2
)–(
3.5
) under the initial assumption (
11.2
).
For the initial data satisfying (
3.8
),
we introduce the so-called effective velocity

\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{u}+2\alpha\boldsymbol{\psi}
.
Utilizing the
time evolution equations
for
\boldsymbol{v}
(
i.e.
, (
11.64
)) and the method of characteristics,
we obtain the
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-estimate of
\boldsymbol{v}
, which yields the
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-estimate of
\boldsymbol{\psi}
(see §
11.1.3
).

3.3.2.
Global-in-time uniform upper bound of the density

In order to extend the local solutions obtained in §
11
to global ones,
one needs to establish the corresponding global uniform estimates.
To this end, we consider system (
1.6
) in spherical coordinates.
The first major task for establishing the desired global estimates for the large-data problem
is to obtain the global uniform upper bound of
\rho
.
It is worth pointing out that we cannot directly obtain the upper bound
of
\rho
from the critical Sobolev embeddings (see Lemma
C.6
in Appendix
C
),
the conservation of total mass, and the BD entropy estimate, since
\rho=\rho(t,\boldsymbol{x})
is a scalar function (see Remark
C.5
).
Thus, we divide this proof for the upper bound of
\rho
into two steps:

•

the upper bound of
\rho
in
B_{1}=\{\boldsymbol{x}:\,|\boldsymbol{x}|<1\}
containing the symmetric center;

•

the upper bound of
\rho
in the domain exterior to
B_{1}
,
i.e.
,
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{1}
.

On one hand, in the exterior domain
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B_{1}
,
(
1.6
) exhibits largely 1-D behaviors. Therefore, it follows from the Sobolev embedding
H^{1}(1,\infty)\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(1,\infty)
,
the conservation of total mass, and the BD entropy estimates
(see Lemma
4.1
in §
4
) that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho\|_{L^{p}(1,\infty)}\leq C_{0}\qquad\,\,\text{for $p\in[1,\infty]$}.

(3.15)

On the other hand, the corresponding analysis in
B_{1}
becomes formidable due to
the coordinate singularity at
\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{0}
indicated by
\frac{1}{r}
in
(
1.6
)
.
As shown in
[
10
,
62
]
, for spherically symmetric classical solutions
of the constant viscous flow,

\rho(t,0)\rightarrow\infty\qquad\text{as $t\rightarrow T_{*}\,$ for some $0<T_{*}<\infty$}.

In order to overcome such an essential difficulty, some new ideas are indeed required.

First, by taking the full advantage of the effective velocity

\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{u}+2\alpha\nabla\log\rho
(its radial projection is
v=u+2\alpha(\log\rho)_{r}
),
the conservation of total mass and the Sobolev embeddings

W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(B_{1})
for
p>n
,
we observe that, for some
p>n
,

\displaystyle\|\rho^{\frac{1}{p}}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}\leq C(p)\big\|\big(\rho^{\frac{1}{p}},\nabla(\rho^{\frac{1}{p}})\big)\big\|_{L^{p}}\leq C(p)\|\rho\|_{L^{1}}^{\frac{1}{p}}+C(p)\big\|\rho^{\frac{1}{p}}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\big\|_{L^{p}},

(3.16)

\displaystyle\implies\,\,\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(0,1)}\leq C(p)+C(p)\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big|(r^{m}\rho)^{\frac{1}{p}}(u,v)\big|_{p}^{p}.

Next, to establish the
L^{p}(I)
-estimates of
(r^{m}\rho)^{\frac{1}{p}}(u,v)
for some
p>n
,
we introduce the second key observation, which stems from the Hardy inequality
(see Lemma
A.3
in Appendix
A
).
Specifically, by treating the radial coordinate
r
as a weight function defined on
[0,1)
,
we are able to derive some new
r
-weighted
L^{p}(0,1)
-estimates of
\rho

based on the BD entropy estimates and the Hardy inequality:

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|r^{K}\rho\|_{L^{p}(0,1)}\leq C(p,K)\qquad\text{for $p\in[1,\infty]$},

(3.17)

where
K>-\frac{1}{p}
(
K>0
for
p=\infty
) if
n=2
and
K\geq 1-\frac{1}{p}
if
n=3
.
It is important to note that the above weighted estimates are distinctly different
from those obtained solely through the BD entropy estimate

\nabla\sqrt{\rho}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))

and the classical Sobolev embeddings

H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(where
q\in[2,\infty)
if
n=2

and
q\in[2,6]
if
n=3
) in the M-D coordinates.
Indeed, these only yield the
L^{p}(B_{1})
-estimates for
\rho
with
p=\frac{q}{2}
which,
under the spherical coordinate transformation, are equivalent to

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|r^{\frac{m}{p}}\rho\|_{L^{p}(0,1)}\leq C(p)\qquad\text{for $p\in[1,\infty)$ if $n=2$ \, and $p\in[1,3]$ if $n=3$}.

(3.18)

Comparing (
3.17
) with (
3.18
), estimate (
3.17
) exhibits broader ranges
for both parameter
p
and the power of the weight function
r
.

Now we briefly outline how to obtain the
L^{p}(I)
-estimates of
(r^{m}\rho)^{\frac{1}{p}}(u,v)
for some
p>n

via the new estimates (
3.17
).
We take the 3-D case as an example, since the 2-D case can be treated similarly.

First, multiply
\eqref{cosingu}_{2}
by
r^{2}|u|^{p-2}u
and integrate the resulting equality over
I
.
Then, via some elaborate analysis on the region segmentation,
we use (
3.15
) to handle the estimates in the exterior domain and obtain

\begin{split}&\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\big|(r^{2}\rho)^{\frac{1}{p}}u\big|_{p}^{p}+\big|(r^{2}\rho)^{\frac{1}{2}}|u|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}u_{r}\big|_{2}^{2}+\big|\rho^{\frac{1}{p}}u\big|_{p}^{p}\\
&\leq C(p)\big(1+\big|(r^{2}\rho)^{\frac{1}{p}}u\big|_{p}^{p}\big)+C(p)\underline{\big\|r^{\frac{p}{p\gamma-p+1}}\rho\big\|_{L^{p\gamma-p+1}(0,1)}^{p\gamma-p+1}}_{:=\mathcal{J}_{1}}.\end{split}

(3.19)

Note that, if
\gamma\in(1,2]
, it follows directly from (
3.17
)
that
\mathcal{J}_{1}\leq C(p)
.
If
\gamma\in(2,3)
, we can first obtain from the Hardy inequality
and
2\alpha\rho_{r}=\rho(v-u)
that, for
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C(p,j)+C(p,j,\epsilon)\underline{\int_{0}^{1}r^{a_{j+1}}\rho^{b_{j+1}}\,\mathrm{d}r}_{:=\mathcal{J}_{2}}

(3.20)

\displaystyle+C(p,j)\epsilon\underline{\|r^{\frac{2}{\gamma-1}}\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(0,1)}^{\gamma-1}}_{:=\mathcal{J}_{3}}\big|(r^{2}\rho)^{\frac{1}{p}}u\big|_{p}^{p}+C(p,j)\epsilon\underline{\big|(r^{2}\rho^{\gamma})^{\frac{1}{p}}v\big|_{p}^{p}}_{:=\mathcal{J}_{4}},

where
(a_{j},b_{j})
satisfy
(a_{1},b_{1})=(p,p\gamma-p+1)
and, for
j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,

a_{j+1}=2(p-1)\Big(\frac{p}{p-1}\Big)^{j}-p+2,\quad b_{j+1}=(p-1)(\gamma-1)\Big(\frac{p}{p-1}\Big)^{j}+\gamma.

(3.21)

For
\mathcal{J}_{4}
, we multiply (
4.15
) (the equation of
v
)
by
r^{2}|v|^{p-2}v
and integrate the resulting equality over
I
.
Employing (
3.15
) to handle the estimates in the exterior domain, we have

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\big|(r^{2}\rho)^{\frac{1}{p}}v\big|_{p}^{p}+\mathcal{J}_{4}\leq C(p)\Big(\mathcal{J}_{3}\,\big|(r^{2}\rho)^{\frac{1}{p}}u\big|_{p}^{p}+\big|\rho^{\frac{1}{p}}u\big|_{p}^{p}\Big).

(3.22)

For
\mathcal{J}_{2}
–
\mathcal{J}_{3}
, since
\gamma\in(2,3)
, we can check that (
3.17
) is applicable to
\mathcal{J}_{3}
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
for all
p\in[2,\infty)
by fixing
j=j_{0}
sufficiently large, so that

\mathcal{J}_{2}+\mathcal{J}_{3}\leq C(p)
.
Therefore, collecting (
3.19
)–(
3.22
), then choosing
\epsilon
sufficiently small,
and using the Grönwall inequality, we can obtain the desired
L^{p}(I)
-estimates
of
(r^{2}\rho)^{\frac{1}{p}}(u,v)
for some
p>3
(see §
4.2
).

3.3.3.
Global-in-time
L^{\infty}(I)
-estimate of the effective velocity

Another major task for the global estimates of the regular solutions of the large-data problems
is to obtain the global
L^{\infty}(I)
-estimate of the effective velocity
v=u+2\alpha(\log\rho)_{r}
.
In fact, based on the
L^{\infty}(I)
-bound of
v
,
we are able to gain control over the derivative of density
\rho_{r}
through
\rho(v-u)
,
which then allows us to establish the required global uniform estimates.

First, by the time evolution equation (
4.15
) for
v

and the method of characteristics, we has

\displaystyle v(t,\eta(t,r))

\displaystyle=v_{0}(r)\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{t}\frac{A\gamma}{2\alpha}\rho^{\gamma-1}(\tau,\eta(\tau,r))\,\mathrm{d}\tau\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{A\gamma}{2\alpha}\int_{0}^{t}(\rho^{\gamma-1}u)(s,\eta(s,r))\!\cdot\!\exp\Big(\!-\!\int_{s}^{t}\frac{A\gamma}{2\alpha}\rho^{\gamma-1}(\tau,\eta(\tau,r))\mathrm{d}\tau\Big)\mathrm{d}s,

where
v_{0}=u_{0}+2\alpha(\log\rho_{0})_{r}
, and
\eta:[0,T]\times I\to I
denotes the flow mapping satisfying
\eta_{t}(t,r)=u(t,\eta(t,r))
and
\eta(0,r)=r
. Taking the
L^{\infty}(I)
-norm above then yields

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|v|_{\infty}\leq|v_{0}|_{\infty}+\frac{A\gamma}{2\alpha}\int_{0}^{T}|\rho^{\gamma-1}u|_{\infty}\,\mathrm{d}s.

(3.23)

Thus, it suffices to control the
L^{1}([0,T];L^{\infty}(I))
-norm of
\rho^{\gamma-1}u
.

To achieve this, we develop an unconventional technique based on the
L^{p}(I)
-estimates
of
\rho^{\frac{1}{p}}u
.
Take
n=3
as an example. Unlike the standard
L^{p}(I)
-energy estimates of
u
for
p\geq 2
,
which involves the multiplication of both sides of
\eqref{cosingu}_{2}
by
r^{2}|u|^{p-2}u

and integration of the resulting equality over
I
,
here we instead multiply
\eqref{cosingu}_{2}
by
|u|^{p-2}u
.
However, this operation inevitably leads to the appearance of some undesired integral terms,
such as
\mathcal{G}_{3}=-\frac{2}{p}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\rho v|u|^{p}}{r}\,\mathrm{d}r

in (
5.6
). Fortunately,
\mathcal{G}_{3}
can be treated by the
L^{\infty}(I)
-norm of
v

and the Young inequality as

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{3}

\displaystyle\leq C(\epsilon,p)|v|_{\infty}^{2}\big|\rho^{\frac{1}{p}}u\big|_{p}^{p}+\epsilon\big|(r^{-2}\rho)^{\frac{1}{p}}u\big|_{p}^{p}\qquad\text{for $\epsilon\in(0,1)$}.

We finally obtain the following type of estimates for
u
(Lemma
4.6
):

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big|\rho^{\frac{1}{p}}u\big|_{p}^{p}+\!\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\Big(\big|\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}|u|^{\frac{p-2}{p}}u_{r}\big|_{2}^{2}\!+\!\big|(r^{-2}\rho)^{\frac{1}{p}}u\big|_{p}^{p}\Big)\mathrm{d}t\leq C(p,T)\Big(\big(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|v|_{\infty}\big)^{2}\!+\!1\Big).

(3.24)

Subsequently, by the Sobolev embedding
W^{1,1}(I)\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(I)
,
we can obtain from the above inequality with suitable fixed
p
and
Lemma
4.6
that, for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}|\rho^{\gamma-1}u|_{\infty}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C(T)\int_{0}^{T}\Big(1+|v|_{\infty}+\big|\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}|u|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}u_{r}\big|_{2}^{\frac{2}{2p-1}}\Big)\mathrm{d}t+C(T)

(3.25)

\displaystyle\leq C(\epsilon,T)\Big(1+\int_{0}^{T}|v|_{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\Big)+\epsilon\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|v|_{\infty}.

Finally, substituting (
3.25
) into (
3.23
) and choosing
\epsilon
sufficiently small,
we obtain the global
L^{\infty}(I)
-estimate of
v
via the Grönwall inequality.
More details are provided in §
5
.

3.3.4.
Non-formation of vacuum states inside the fluids in finite time and the pointwise estimate of the density

Based on the global upper bound of
\rho
and the
L^{\infty}(I)
-estimate of
v
,
we can show that the cavitation does not form inside the fluids in finite time,
provided that no vacuum states are presented initially inside the fluids.
Moreover, based on the specific decay rate of
\rho_{0}(r)
in
I
,
we can derive the corresponding pointwise estimates for
\rho(t,r)
in
[0,T]\times I
.

Specifically, let
T>0
be any finite time.
We first multiply
\eqref{cosingu}_{2}
by
\rho^{-1}u
and integrate the resulting equality over
I
.
Then it follows from the
L^{\infty}(I)
-estimates of
(\rho,v)
that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|u|_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\Big|\big(u_{r},\frac{u}{r}\big)\Big|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C(T),

(3.26)

which, together with the
L^{\infty}(I)
-estimate of
v=u+2\alpha(\log\rho)_{r}
, yields
