\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\|\diver\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(Z(\boldsymbol{w}))_{tt}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

(11.85)

Here, we temporarily assume that the above energy
equality holds. The rigorous proof of (
11.85
) will be given in
Step 3 below.

Then it follows from (
11.83
), (
11.85
), and the Young inequality that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\|\diver\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

(11.86)

\displaystyle\leq Cc_{3}^{14\gamma+2}t\big(\|(\nabla\boldsymbol{w}_{tt},\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{w}_{t},\boldsymbol{w}_{tt})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+1\big)+\frac{C}{t}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Multiplying the above by
t
and integrating the resulting inequality over
[\tau,t]
(
\tau>0
), we obtain from (
11.70
) and (
11.80
) that, for all
t\in[\tau,T_{3}]
,

\displaystyle t\|\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{\tau}^{t}s\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

(11.87)

\displaystyle\leq\tau\|\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+Cc_{3}^{14\gamma+2}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}s\big(s\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}_{tt}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+c_{4}^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s+t^{2}\Big)+C\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\tau\|\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C\big(c_{3}^{14\gamma+2}c_{4}^{2}t+c_{3}^{14\gamma+2}c_{4}^{2}t^{2}+c_{0}^{4}\big)\leq\tau\|\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+Cc_{0}^{4}.

For
\tau\|\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}
, by (
11.80
) and Lemma
A.6
,
there exists a sequence
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
such that

\tau_{k}\to 0,\quad\,\tau_{k}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}(\tau_{k})\|_{L^{2}}\to 0\qquad\,\,\text{as $k\to\infty$}.

Thus, setting
\tau=\tau_{k}
and letting
\tau_{k}\to 0
in (
11.87
) yield that, for all
t\in[0,T_{3}]
,

t\|\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}s\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq Cc_{0}^{4}.

(11.88)

Next, it follows from (
11.38
), (
11.70
), (
11.80
),
(
11.82
), and (
11.88
) that, for all
t\in[0,T_{3}]
,

\displaystyle t\|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ct\big(\|\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+c_{3}^{6}\|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{w}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}+c_{3}^{14\gamma-6}\big)\leq C\big(c_{0}^{4}+c_{3}^{7}\sqrt{t}\big)\leq Cc_{0}^{4},

(11.89)

\displaystyle t\|\nabla^{4}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ct\big(\|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+c_{2}^{14\gamma-6}c_{3}^{2}\big)\leq Cc_{0}^{4}.

Finally, we establish the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
-estimate of
\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{u}_{t}
. First, it follows from (
11.21
), (
11.70
), Lemmas
11.7
–
11.8
and
A.1
, and the Hölder inequality that

\displaystyle\|\nabla(Z(\boldsymbol{w}))_{t}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\|(\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}_{t},\nabla\boldsymbol{w}_{t})\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{t},\boldsymbol{w}_{t})\|_{L^{4}}\|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\|_{L^{4}}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+C\big(\|(\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi},\nabla\boldsymbol{w})\|_{L^{4}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}_{t}\|_{L^{4}}\!+\!\|(\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{w})\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{w}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}\big)\!+\!\|\nabla^{2}\phi_{t}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\|(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{t},\boldsymbol{w}_{t})\|_{H^{1}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\|_{H^{2}}+\|(\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi},\nabla\boldsymbol{w})\|_{H^{1}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}_{t}\|_{H^{1}}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+C\big(\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\boldsymbol{w}\|_{H^{2}}\big)\|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{w}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla^{2}\phi_{t}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Cc_{3}^{2}\|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{w}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}+Cc_{3}^{7\gamma-1}.

Next, we obtain from the above, (
11.37
), (
11.70
), and the classical regularity theory for elliptic equations in Lemma
A.13
that

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\big\|\big(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{tt},\nabla(Z(\boldsymbol{w}))_{t}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\big(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\|_{L^{2}}+c_{3}^{2}\|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{w}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}+c_{3}^{7\gamma-1}\big),

which, along with (
11.88
), implies that, for all
t\in[0,T_{3}]
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s\|\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{t}s\big(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+c_{3}^{4}\|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{w}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+c_{3}^{14\gamma-2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{t}s\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+Cc_{3}^{6}t+Cc_{3}^{14\gamma-2}t^{2}\leq Cc_{0}^{4}.

3. Justification of the energy equality (
11.85
).
Let
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be any vector valued test function. We can first multiply (
11.27
) by
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
and then integrate the resulting equality over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to obtain

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\cdot\boldsymbol{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=-\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{t}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\varphi}+\diver\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\diver\boldsymbol{\varphi})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(Z(\boldsymbol{w}))_{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

Differentiate the above with respect to
t
. Then it follows from
the calculation of (
11.86
) and
a priori
assumptions
(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{tt},\nabla\phi_{tt},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{tt})\in L^{2}([\tau,T^{\prime}];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
shown in Lemma
11.6
that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\cdot\boldsymbol{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\leq F(t)\|\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\|_{H^{1}}

(11.90)

holds for
t\in[\tau,T^{\prime}]
and for some positive function
F(t)\in L^{2}(\tau,T^{\prime})
. According to Lemma 1.1 on
[
77
, page 250]
, the above equality implies that
\boldsymbol{u}_{ttt}\in L^{2}([\tau,T^{\prime}];H^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
. (
11.85
) thus follows directly from the following identity, due to Lemma
A.10
:

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=2\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{ttt},\boldsymbol{u}_{tt}\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\qquad\text{for {\it a.e.} $t\in(\tau,T^{\prime})$}.

The proof of Lemma
11.9
is completed.
∎

Now, choose
T^{*}

and constants
c_{i}

(i=1,2,3,4)
as

\displaystyle T^{*}=T_{3},\qquad c_{1}=C^{\frac{1}{2}}c_{0},\qquad c_{2}=C^{\frac{2}{4-n}}c_{1}^{\frac{28\gamma-12}{4-n}}=C^{\frac{14\gamma-4}{4-n}}c_{0}^{\frac{28\gamma-12}{4-n}},

\displaystyle c_{3}=c_{4}=C^{\frac{2}{4-n}}c_{2}^{\frac{28\gamma-12}{4-n}}=C^{\frac{8-2n+(14\gamma-4)(28\gamma-12)}{(4-n)^{2}}}c_{0}^{\frac{(28\gamma-12)^{2}}{(4-n)^{2}}}.

Then it follows from Lemmas
11.7
–
11.9
that, for
t\in[0,T^{*}]
,

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\big(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}^{2},

(11.91)

\displaystyle\|(\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u}_{t})(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|(\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{t})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}^{2},

\displaystyle\|(\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{t})(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|(\nabla^{4}\boldsymbol{u},\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{tt})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}^{2},

\displaystyle t\|(\nabla^{4}\boldsymbol{u},\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{tt})(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}s\|(\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{u}_{t},\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{tt})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq c_{4}^{2},

\displaystyle\big\|\phi^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}(t)\big\|_{L^{1}}+\|(\phi,\boldsymbol{\psi})(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\big\|(\nabla\phi,\phi_{t})(t)\big\|_{H^{2}}+\big\|(\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{t})(t)\big\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq Cc_{3}^{7\gamma-1},

\displaystyle\|\phi_{tt}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\big\|(\nabla\phi_{tt},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{tt})\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq Cc_{3}^{14\gamma}.

11.2.3.
Proof of Theorem
3.2

With the help of the uniform estimates (
11.91
), the existence and uniqueness of the
3
-order regular solutions can be derived by an argument similar to that in Steps 1–2 in §
11.1.3
. To show that the
3
-order regular solutions are actually classical ones, we can follow the same argument as in Steps 2–3 in §
9.2
. We omit the details here for brevity.

11.2.4.
Proof of Theorem
3.4

Theorem
3.4
can be proved by the same argument as in §
11.1.4
. We omit the details here for brevity.

Appendix A
Some Basic Lemmas

This appendix lists some useful lemmas that have been used frequently in the previous sections. The first is the classical Sobolev embedding theorem.

Lemma A.1

(
[
1
]
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}

(n\in\mathbb{N}^{*})
be a domain with smooth boundary, and
f\in W^{k,p}(\Omega)
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
.

\mathrm{(i)}

Let
kp\leq n
. Then
W^{k,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{s}(\Omega)
for all
s\in\big[p,\frac{np}{n-kp}\big]
if
kp<n
and all
s\in[p,\infty)
if
kp=n
, and there exists a constant
C_{1}>0
depending only on
(k,p,s,n,\Omega)
such that

\|f\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}\leq C_{1}\|f\|_{W^{k,p}(\Omega)}.

In particular, if
kp<n
,
s=\frac{np}{n-kp}
,
and
f\in D^{k,p}\cap L^{\frac{np}{n-kp}}
, then

\|f\|_{L^{\frac{np}{n-kp}}(\Omega)}\leq C_{1}\|\nabla^{k}f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

(A.1)

\mathrm{(ii)}

Let
(k,p)=(n,1)
. Then
W^{n,1}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow C(\bar{\Omega})
and there exists a constant
C_{2}>0
depending only on
(n,\Omega)
such that

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C_{2}\|f\|_{W^{n,1}(\Omega)}.

\mathrm{(iii)}

Let
kp>n
. Then
W^{k,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow C^{\ell}(\bar{\Omega})
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
0\leq\ell<k-n/p
, and there exists a constant
C_{3}>0
depending only on
(k,p,\ell,n,\Omega)
such that

\max_{0\leq j\leq\ell}\|\nabla^{j}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C_{3}\|f\|_{W^{k,p}(\Omega)},

where
C^{\ell}(\bar{\Omega})

(\ell\in\mathbb{N},\,C(\bar{\Omega})=C^{0}(\bar{\Omega}))
denotes the space of all functions
f
for which
\nabla^{j}f

(0\leq j\leq\ell)
are bounded and uniformly continuous in
\Omega
. In particular, the following inequality holds for
f=f(r)\in H^{1}(0,R)

(R>0)
:

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(0,R)}^{2}\leq(1+R^{-1})\|f\|_{L^{2}(0,R)}^{2}+\|f_{r}\|_{L^{2}(0,R)}^{2}.

(A.2)

Moreover, if
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then the above constants
(C_{1},C_{2},C_{3})
are independent of
\Omega
.

Proof.

We only give the proof for inequality (
A.2
). It follows from the fundamental theorem of calculus and the Young inequality that, for all
0\leq\tilde{r}<r\leq R
,

\displaystyle|f(r)|^{2}

\displaystyle=|f(\tilde{r})|^{2}+2\int_{\tilde{r}}^{r}f(z)f_{r}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq|f(\tilde{r})|^{2}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(0,R)}^{2}+\|f_{r}\|_{L^{2}(0,R)}^{2}.

Then integrating the above over
[0,R]
with respect to
\tilde{r}
yields

\displaystyle R|f(r)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|_{L^{2}(0,R)}^{2}+R\|f\|_{L^{2}(0,R)}^{2}+R\|f_{r}\|_{L^{2}(0,R)}^{2},

which leads to

|f(r)|^{2}\leq(1+R^{-1})\|f\|_{L^{2}(0,R)}^{2}+\|f_{r}\|_{L^{2}(0,R)}^{2}\qquad\text{for all $r\in[0,R]$}.

∎

The second lemma concerns the well-known Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality.

Lemma A.2

(
[
66
]
)
.

Assume that
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap D^{\ell,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
. Let real numbers
(\epsilon,b)
and natural numbers
(n,\ell,j)
satisfy

\frac{1}{{b}}=\frac{j}{n}+\Big(\frac{1}{q}-\frac{\ell}{n}\Big)\epsilon+\frac{1-\epsilon}{p},\qquad\frac{j}{\ell}\leq\epsilon\leq 1.

Then
f\in D^{j,{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and there exists
C>0
depending only on
(\ell,n,j,p,q,\epsilon)
such that

\|\nabla^{j}f\|_{L^{{b}}}\leq C\|\nabla^{\ell}f\|_{L^{q}}^{\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}}^{1-\epsilon}.

Moreover, if
j=0
,
\ell q<n
, and
p=\infty
, then it is necessary to make the additional assumption that either f tends to zero at infinity or that f lies in
L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some finite
s>0
;
if
1<q<\infty
and
\ell-j-n/q
is a non-negative integer, then it is necessary to assume also that
\epsilon\neq 1
.

The third lemma is on the Hardy inequality.

Lemma A.3

(
[
9
,
67
]
)
.

The following statements hold
:

\mathrm{(i)}

Let
q\in[2,\infty)
and
b>0
. Assume that
f=f(r)
is a function defined on
[0,b]
such that
r^{K+1+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}(f,f_{r})\in L^{q}(0,b)
for some
p\in[q,\infty]
and
K>-\frac{1}{p}

(K>0
if
p=\infty)
. Then
r^{K}f\in L^{p}(0,b)
and the following inequalities hold
:

\displaystyle\|r^{K}f\|_{L^{p}(0,b)}

\displaystyle\leq C(K,p,q,b)\big\|r^{K+1+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}(f,f_{r})\big\|_{L^{q}(0,b)}\qquad\text{if }p\in[q,\infty),

\displaystyle\|r^{K}f\|_{L^{\infty}(0,b)}

\displaystyle\leq C(K,q,b)\big\|r^{K+1-\frac{1}{q}}(f,f_{r})\big\|_{L^{q}(0,b)}\qquad\qquad\text{if }p=\infty,

where
C(K,p,q,b)
and
C(K,q,b)
are positive constants depending only on
(K,p,q,b)
and
(K,q,b)
, respectively. In particular, if
r^{K+1-\frac{1}{q}}(f,f_{r})\in L^{q}(0,b)
for some
K>0
, then
r^{K}f\in C([0,b])
.

\mathrm{(ii)}

Let
f=f(r)\in W^{1,p}(0,\infty)
for some
p\in(1,\infty)
with
f|_{r=0}=0
. Then
\frac{f}{r}\in L^{p}(0,\infty)
and the following inequality holds
:

\Big\|\frac{f}{r}\Big\|_{L^{p}(0,\infty)}\leq C(p)\|f_{r}\|_{L^{p}(0,\infty)}.

Proof.

For brevity, when giving the proof for (i), we only consider
b=1
,
since the proof for general
b>0
can be derived analogously. We divide the proof into five steps.

1. Proof of (i) when
p=q
.
First, for
K>-\frac{1}{q}
, it follows from integration by parts that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK}|f|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{qK+1}\Big(|f|^{q}(1)-q\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK+1}|f|^{q-2}ff_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big),

(A.3)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK+q}|f|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{qK+q+1}\Big(|f|^{q}(1)-q\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK+q+1}|f|^{q-2}ff_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big).

(A.4)

Combining (
A.3
)–(
A.4
) with the Hölder and Young inequalities
leads to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK}|f|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{qK+q+1}{qK+1}\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK+q}|f|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r+\frac{q}{qK+1}\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK+q+1}|f|^{q-2}ff_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\quad\,-\frac{q}{qK+1}\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK+1}|f|^{q-2}ff_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq C(K,q)\Big(\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK+q}|f|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK+q}|f_{r}|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK}|f|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r,

which implies that

\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK}|f|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r\leq C(K,q)\Big(\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK+q}|f|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK+q}|f_{r}|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big).

(A.5)

2. Proof of (i) when
p=\infty
.

Next, for any
K>0
, since
r^{K}f|_{r=0}=0
, it follows from (
A.5
), Lemma
A.4
, and the Young inequality that

\displaystyle\|r^{K}f\|_{L^{\infty}(0,1)}^{q}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}|(r^{qK}|f|^{q})_{r}|\,\mathrm{d}r\leq qK\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK-1}|f|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r+q\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK}|f|^{q-1}|f_{r}|\,\mathrm{d}r

(A.6)

\displaystyle\leq C(K,q)\Big(\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK-1}|f|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK+q-1}|f_{r}|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C(K,q)\Big(\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK+q-1}|f|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{1}r^{qK+q-1}|f_{r}|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big).

3. Proof of (i) when
p\in(q,\infty)
.
Finally, for
K>-\frac{1}{p}
, via integration by parts,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}r^{pK}|f|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{pK+1}\Big(|f|^{p}(1)-p\int_{0}^{1}r^{pK+1}|f|^{p-2}ff_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big),

(A.7)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}r^{pK+p}|f|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{pK+p+1}\Big(|f|^{p}(1)-p\int_{0}^{1}r^{pK+p+1}|f|^{p-2}ff_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big).

(A.8)

By (
A.5
)–(
A.6
) and the Hölder inequality, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}r^{pK+1}|f|^{p-2}ff_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\big\|r^{K+\frac{1}{p}}f\big\|_{L^{\infty}(0,1)}^{p-q}\big\|r^{K+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}f\big\|_{L^{q}(0,1)}\big\|r^{K+1+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}f_{r}\big\|_{L^{q}(0,1)}

\displaystyle\leq C(K,p)\Big(\big\|r^{K+1+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}f\big\|_{L^{q}(0,1)}^{p}+\big\|r^{K+1+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}f_{r}\big\|_{L^{q}(0,1)}^{p}\Big).

Thus, based on the above inequality and the similar discussions in Step 1, we conclude the inequality
from (
A.7
)–(
A.8
).

4.
Now we show that, if
r^{K+1-\frac{1}{q}}(f,f_{r})\in L^{q}(0,b)
for some
K>0
and
q\in[2,\infty)
, then
r^{K}f\in C([0,b])
.
For brevity, we give the proof only when
q=2
and
b=1
.
For any
f
satisfying
r^{K+\frac{1}{2}}(f,f_{r})\in L^{2}(0,1)
,
according to Theorem 7.2 in
[
53
]
,
there exists a sequence of
\{f^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}\subset C^{\infty}([0,1])
such that

\|r^{K+\frac{1}{2}}(f^{\epsilon}-f,f_{r}^{\epsilon}-f_{r})\|_{L^{2}(0,1)}\to 0\qquad\,\,\text{as $\epsilon\to 0$}.

Then, following the same calculation (
A.6
), we obtain that
\{r^{K}f^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{\infty}(0,1)
. Since
\{r^{K}f^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}\subset C^{\infty}([0,1])
, there exists a limit
g\in C([0,1])
such that

r^{K}f^{\epsilon}\to g\ \text{ uniformly on $[0,1]$}\qquad\text{as $\epsilon\to 0$.}

On the other hand, since
f^{\epsilon}\to f
in
H^{1}(\sigma,1)

as
\epsilon\to 0
for any fixed
\sigma>0
, we obtain from Lemma
A.1
that, for any fixed
\sigma>0
,
f^{\epsilon}\to f
uniformly on
[\sigma,1]
as
\epsilon\to 0
so that

r^{K}f^{\epsilon}\to r^{K}f\ \text{ pointwisely on $(0,1]$}\qquad\text{as $\epsilon\to 0$.}

The uniqueness of the limit thus yields
r^{K}f=g
for
a.e.

r\in(0,1)
and
r^{K}f\in C([0,1])
.

5. Proof of (ii).
We first consider
f\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}((0,\infty))
.
By integration by parts, we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{f}{r}\Big\|_{L^{p}(0,\infty)}^{p}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|f|^{p}}{r^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}r=\frac{p}{p-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r^{p-1}}|f|^{p-2}ff_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\leq C(p)\Big\|\frac{f}{r}\Big\|_{L^{p}(0,\infty)}^{p-1}\|f_{r}\|_{L^{p}(0,\infty)}.

Applying the Young inequality yields

\Big\|\frac{f}{r}\Big\|_{L^{p}(0,\infty)}\leq C(p)\|f_{r}\|_{L^{p}(0,\infty)}.

(A.9)

Now, for any
f\in W^{1,p}(0,\infty)
with
f|_{r=0}=0
, there exists a sequence of

\{f^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}\subset C_{\rm c}^{\infty}((0,\infty))
such that
f^{\epsilon}\to f
in
W^{1,p}(0,\infty)
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Then (
A.9
) implies that
\{\frac{f^{\epsilon}}{r}\}_{\epsilon>0}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{p}(0,\infty)
, converging to some limit
g\in L^{p}(0,\infty)
as
\epsilon\to 0
:

\frac{f^{\epsilon}}{r}\to g\,\,\text{ in $L^{p}(0,\infty)$}\qquad\text{as $\epsilon\to 0$}.

On the other hand, thanks to Lemma
A.1
,
f^{\epsilon}\to f
pointwisely on
(0,\infty)
so that

\frac{f^{\epsilon}}{r}\to\frac{f}{r}\,\,\text{ for {\it a.e.} $r\in(0,\infty)$}\qquad\text{as $\epsilon\to 0$}.

Therefore, the uniqueness of the limit yields
\frac{f}{r}=g\in L^{p}(0,\infty)
.
∎

The fourth lemma is on the fundamental theorem of calculus.

Lemma A.4

(
[
70
]
)
.

Let
f(r)\in W^{1,1}(a,\infty)
for some
a\in[0,\infty)
. Then,
for any
r,r_{0}\in[a,\infty)
,

f(r)=-\int_{r}^{\infty}f^{\prime}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z=f(r_{0})+\int_{r_{0}}^{r}f^{\prime}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z.

(A.10)

Moreover,
f(r)\to 0
as
r\to\infty
.

Proof.

First, Lemma
A.1
implies that
f\in C([a,\infty))
,
and thus
f(r)
is well-defined for each
r\in[a,\infty)
.
Next, it suffices to check the first formula in (
A.10
),
since the second one can be derived in the same way.

For any
f\in W^{1,1}(a,\infty)
with
a\in[0,\infty)
, there exists a sequence of

\{f^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}\subset C_{\rm c}^{\infty}([a,\infty))
satisfying

f^{\epsilon}\to f\ \ \text{ in $W^{1,1}(a,\infty)$}\qquad\text{as $\epsilon\to 0$}.

(A.11)

This, together with Lemma
A.1
, implies also that

f^{\epsilon}\to f\ \ \text{ in $L^{\infty}(a,\infty)$}\qquad\text{as $\epsilon\to 0$}.

(A.12)

Thus, based on (
A.11
)–(
A.12
) and

f^{\epsilon}(r)=-\int_{r}^{\infty}(f^{\epsilon})^{\prime}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z,

we let
\epsilon\to 0
in the above equality to obtain the desired formula in (
A.10
).

Finally, let
\chi_{E}
be the characteristic function defined on the set
E\subset[a,\infty)
,
i.e.
,
\chi_{E}=1
on
E
and
\chi_{E}=0
on
[a,\infty)\backslash E
. Note that
f^{\prime}
is a measurable function defined
a.e.
on
[a,\infty)
due to
f^{\prime}\in L^{1}(a,\infty)
. Then it follows from (
A.10
), the fact that

\lim_{r\to\infty}f^{\prime}(z)\chi_{[r,\infty)}(z)=0\qquad\text{for {\it a.e.} $z\in[a,\infty)$},

and the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem that

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}f(r)

\displaystyle=-\lim_{r\to\infty}\int_{r}^{\infty}f^{\prime}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z=-\lim_{r\to\infty}\int_{a}^{\infty}f^{\prime}(z)\chi_{[r,\infty)}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=-\int_{a}^{\infty}\lim_{r\to\infty}f^{\prime}(z)\chi_{[r,\infty)}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z=0.

∎

The fifth lemma is the well-known Fatou lemma which can be found in
[
70
]
.

Lemma A.5

(
[
70
]
)
.

Given a measure space
(V,\mathcal{F},\nu)
and a set
X\in\mathcal{F}
, let
\{f_{n}\}
be a sequence of
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}})
-measurable non-negative functions
f_{n}:X\rightarrow[0,\infty]
. Define the function
f:X\rightarrow[0,\infty]
by setting

f(x)=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}f_{n}(x)\qquad\,\,\mbox{ for {\it a.e.} $x\in X$}.

Then
f
is
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}})
-measurable and

\int_{X}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{X}f_{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu.

The sixth lemma is used to obtain the time-weighted estimates of the velocity.

Lemma A.6

(
[
3
]
)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}

(n\in\mathbb{N}^{*})
be any set and
f\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(E))
. Then there exists a sequence
\{t_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
such that

t_{k}\rightarrow 0,\quad\ t_{k}\|f(t_{k})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(E)}\rightarrow 0\qquad\,\,\text{as $k\rightarrow\infty$}.

Proof.

Let
F(t)=\|f(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O})}
. Clearly,
0\leq F(t)\in L^{1}(0,T)
.
Then it suffices to show that, for any
k\geq 1
, there exists
t_{k}\in\big(0,\frac{T}{1+k}\big)
such that

t_{k}F(t_{k})<\frac{1}{k}\to 0\qquad\text{as $k\rightarrow\infty$}.

Assume by contradiction that there exists some
k_{0}\geq 1
such that, for any
t\in\big(0,\frac{T}{1+k_{0}}\big)
,

tF(t)\geq\frac{1}{k_{0}}
.
Then

\int_{0}^{T}F(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\geq\frac{1}{k_{0}}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{s}\,\mathrm{d}s=\infty.

This contradicts with the fact
F(t)\in L^{1}(0,T)
.
Therefore, the claim holds.
∎

The seventh lemma shows that any spherical symmetric vector field must vanish at the symmetric center.

Lemma A.7
.

Let
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(|\boldsymbol{x}|)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{|\boldsymbol{x}|}
be a continuous and spherically symmetric vector field defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{0})=\boldsymbol{0}
.

Proof.

Indeed, since
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(|\boldsymbol{x}|)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{|\boldsymbol{x}|}
is spherically symmetric, we see that

\boldsymbol{f}(\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{x})=(\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{f})(\boldsymbol{x})
for all
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and

\mathcal{O}\in\mathrm{SO}(n)
.
Taking
\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{0}
leads to

\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{0})=(\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{f})(\boldsymbol{0})\qquad\text{for all $\mathcal{O}\in\mathrm{SO}(n)$}.

(A.13)

Next, choosing a rotation
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{1}
by 180 degrees
about an axis perpendicular to
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{0})
,
that is,
(\mathcal{O}_{1}\boldsymbol{f})(\boldsymbol{0})=-\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{0})
,
we obtain from (
A.13
) that
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{0})=-\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{0})
,
which implies that
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{0})=\boldsymbol{0}
.
∎

The eighth lemma is on the Mihlin-Hörmander multiplier theorem.

Lemma A.8

(
[
33
]
)
.

Let
\varphi(\boldsymbol{\omega})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

(n\in\mathbb{N}^{*})

satisfy that there exists some constant
A_{0}>0
such that

\Big(\int_{R<|\boldsymbol{\omega}|<2R}\left|\partial_{\omega}^{\varsigma}\varphi(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\omega}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq A_{0}R^{\frac{n}{2}-|\varsigma|}

for all
R>0
and all multi-indices
0\leq|\varsigma|\leq\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1
, where
\lfloor q\rfloor
denotes the integer part of the real number
q
.
Denote by
(\mathcal{F}[\,\cdot\,],\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\,\cdot\,])
the Fourier transform and the inverse Fourier transform, respectively, and define the operator
\varphi(D)
by
\varphi(D)f:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big[\varphi(\boldsymbol{\omega})\mathcal{F}[f](\boldsymbol{\omega})\big]
. Then, for all
p\in(1,\infty)
, there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
(n,p,A_{0})
and
\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
such that

\|\varphi(D)f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\qquad\text{for all $f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$}.

The next lemma is on the well-known Chebyshev inequality.

Lemma A.9

(
[
33
]
)
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty)
and
f=f(\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Let

S^{\sigma}:=\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\,|f(\boldsymbol{x})|\geq\sigma\},

and let
\chi_{S^{\sigma}}=\chi_{S^{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{x})
be the characteristic function with respect to
S^{\sigma}
. Then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\chi_{S^{\sigma}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\leq\frac{\|f\chi_{S^{\sigma}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}}{\sigma^{p}}\qquad\,\,\text{for any $\sigma>0$}.

In particular, if
f(\boldsymbol{x})=f(r)
is spherically symmetric and
S^{\sigma}=\{r\in I:\,|f(r)|\geq\sigma\}
, then

\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi_{S^{\sigma}}r^{m}\,\mathrm{d}r\leq\frac{\big|r^{\frac{m}{p}}f\chi_{S^{\sigma}}\big|_{p}^{p}}{\sigma^{p}}\qquad\,\,\text{for any $\sigma>0$}.

The following lemma is on the evolution triple embedding.

Lemma A.10

(
[
24
]
)
.

Let
T>0
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
n\geq 2
.
Suppose that
f\in L^{2}([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and
f_{t}\in L^{2}([0,T];H^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
.
Then
f\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, and the mapping
:

t\mapsto|f(t)|_{2}^{2}
is absolutely continuous
with

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}=2\left<f_{t},f\right>_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\qquad\text{for {\it a.e.} $t\in(0,T)$}.

Moreover, if additionally
f\in L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, then
f\in C([0,T];L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
for
q\in[2,\infty)
if
n=2
and for
q\in[2,\frac{2n}{n-2})
if
n\geq 3
.

The following auxiliary lemma is used to show some equivalent norms for a function
f

satisfying
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\nabla\log f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Lemma A.11
.

Let
n=2,3
, and let
f
be a spherically symmetric scalar function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. If
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\nabla\log f\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and, additionally,
\nabla\log f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
if
n=3
, then

\mathrm{(i)}

f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
p\in(1,\infty]
, and
\nabla\log f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
if
n=2
;

\mathrm{(ii)}

\nabla(f^{\nu})\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
\nu\in[\frac{1}{2},\infty)
.

If
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\nabla\log f\in D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
(i)–(ii)
hold and

\mathrm{(iii)}

\nabla\log f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and
\nabla(f^{\nu})\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
\nu\in[\frac{1}{2},\infty)
.

Proof.

Note that, since
f
is a spherically symmetric scalar function,
\nabla\log f
is a spherically symmetric vector function.

For (i), we obtain from Lemma
C.4
in Appendix
C
that

\|\nabla\log f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\|\nabla\log f\|_{D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C.

(A.14)

Then it follows from (
A.14
), Lemma
A.2
, and the Hölder inequality that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{L^{1}}^{\frac{4-n}{n+4}}\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{2n}{n+4}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{L^{1}}^{\frac{4-n}{n+4}}\big(\|f|\nabla\log f|^{2}\|_{L^{2}}+\|f\nabla^{2}\log f\|_{L^{2}}\big)^{\frac{2n}{n+4}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{L^{1}}^{\frac{4-n}{n+4}}\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{2n}{n+4}}\|\nabla\log f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{4n}{n+4}}+C\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2n}{n+4}}\|\nabla^{2}\log f\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{2n}{n+4}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{L^{1}}^{\frac{4}{n+4}}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{n}{n+4}}\|\nabla\log f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{4n}{n+4}}+C\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2n}{n+4}}\|\nabla^{2}\log f\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{2n}{n+4}},

which, along with the Young inequality that

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{1}}\|\nabla\log f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{n}+C\|\nabla^{2}\log f\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{2n}{4-n}}.

(A.15)

Thus,
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
and (i) follows easily from this estimate and
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

For (ii), we need the following identities:

(f^{\nu})_{x_{i}}=\nu f^{\nu}(\log f)_{x_{i}},\qquad(f^{\nu})_{x_{i}x_{j}}=\nu^{2}f^{\nu}(\log f)_{x_{i}}(\log f)_{x_{j}}+\nu f^{\nu}(\log f)_{x_{i}x_{j}}.

Then these, together with
\nu\in[\frac{1}{2},\infty)
and the Hölder inequality, yield that

\displaystyle\|\nabla(f^{\nu})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f^{\nu}\nabla\log f\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|f\|^{\nu}_{L^{2\nu}}\|\nabla\log f\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C,

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}(f^{\nu})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f^{\nu}|\nabla\log f|^{2}\|_{L^{2}}+C\|f^{\nu}\nabla^{2}\log f\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|^{\nu}_{L^{2\nu}}\|\nabla\log f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+C\|f\|^{\nu}_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla^{2}\log f\|_{L^{2}}\leq C.

Finally, for
\mathrm{(iii)}
, we first obtain from Lemmas
A.2
and
C.2
that

\displaystyle\|\nabla\log\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\nabla\log\rho\|_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla\log\rho\|_{D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{\frac{1}{2}}

(A.16)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\nabla\log\rho\|_{D^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla\log\rho\|_{D^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C.

Since

\displaystyle(f^{\nu})_{x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}}

\displaystyle=\nu^{3}f^{\nu}(\log f)_{x_{i}}(\log f)_{x_{j}}(\log f)_{x_{k}}+\nu^{2}f^{\nu}(\log f)_{x_{i}x_{k}}(\log f)_{x_{j}}

\displaystyle+\!\nu^{2}f^{\nu}(\log f)_{x_{i}}(\log f)_{x_{j}x_{k}}\!+\!\nu^{2}f^{\nu}(\log f)_{x_{k}}(\log f)_{x_{i}x_{j}}\!+\!\nu f^{\nu}(\log f)_{x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}},

it follows from
\nu\in[\frac{1}{2},\infty)
, (
A.14
), (
A.16
),
and the Hölder inequality that

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{3}(f^{\nu})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f^{\nu}|\nabla\log f|^{3}\|_{L^{2}}+C\|f^{\nu}\nabla^{3}\log f\|_{L^{2}}+C\|f^{\nu}|\nabla\log f||\nabla^{2}\log f|\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|^{\nu}_{L^{2\nu}}\|\nabla\log f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{3}+C\|f\|^{\nu}_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla^{3}\log f\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+C\|f\|^{\nu}_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\log f\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla^{2}\log f\|_{L^{2}}\leq C.

∎

Besides, to establish the
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
-estimates for transport equations, it also
requires the following commutator estimates to justify the process of integration by parts. Specifically, consider the following general transport equation:

g_{t}+\diver(\boldsymbol{w}g)=f.

(A.17)

Lemma A.12

(
[
57
]
)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
T>0
. Assume that

f\in L^{1}([0,T];L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad\boldsymbol{w}\in L^{1}([0,T];W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad g\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),

for some
p\in[1,\infty]
, satisfying (
A.17
) in the sense of distributions. Denote by
\{\omega_{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}
the standard mollifies on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and define the commutator
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{w},g)
by

\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{w},g):=\diver(\boldsymbol{w}g)*\omega_{\epsilon}-\diver(\boldsymbol{w}(g*\omega_{\epsilon})).

Then there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
(\epsilon,\boldsymbol{w},g)
such that

\big\|\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{w},g)\big\|_{L^{1}([0,T];L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{w}\|_{L^{1}([0,T];W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))}.

(A.18)

In addition,
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{w},g)\to 0
in
L^{1}([0,T];L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
as
\epsilon\to 0
if
p<\infty
. In particular, for any
p\in[2,\infty)
and for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
,

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|g\|^{p}_{L^{p}}=-(p-1)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|g|^{p}\diver\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+p\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|g|^{p-2}gf\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

(A.19)

Proof.

The proof of (
A.18
) can be found in
[
57
, Chapter 2]
.
We only give the proof for (
A.19
). First, in view of (
A.17
), we see that, for all
\zeta=\zeta(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in C^{\infty}_{\rm c}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\zeta_{t}g^{\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\zeta\Big(\underline{\diver(\boldsymbol{w}g^{\epsilon})-f^{\epsilon}+\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{w},g)}_{:=\mathcal{R}_{\epsilon}}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{d}t.

(A.20)

where
F^{\epsilon}:=F*\omega_{\epsilon}
for any function
F
.
Clearly,
g^{\epsilon}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
,
and
g^{\epsilon}
is smooth in spatial coordinates for each
\epsilon>0
.
Then it follows from the above, (
A.18
), and the Hölder inequality that
\mathcal{R}_{\epsilon}\in L^{1}([0,T];L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, which, along with the definition of weak derivatives, implies that
g^{\epsilon}
admits the weak derivative
g^{\epsilon}_{t}\in L^{1}([0,T];L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, and

g^{\epsilon}_{t}=-\mathcal{R}_{\epsilon}=-\diver(\boldsymbol{w}g^{\epsilon})+f^{\epsilon}-\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{w},g)\qquad\text{for {\it a.e.} $(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}$}.

Now multiplying above by
p|g^{\epsilon}|^{p-2}g^{\epsilon}
with
p\in[2,\infty)
and integrating the resulting equality over
[\tau,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
0\leq\tau<t\leq T
yield

\displaystyle\|g^{\epsilon}(t)\|^{p}_{L^{p}}+(p-1)\int_{\tau}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|g^{\epsilon}|^{p}\diver\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\|g^{\epsilon}(\tau)\|^{p}_{L^{p}}+p\int_{\tau}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|g^{\epsilon}|^{p-2}g^{\epsilon}\big(f^{\epsilon}-\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{w},g)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}.

Taking the limit
\epsilon\to 0
, together with
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{w},g)\to 0
in
L^{1}([0,T];L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, yields that

\displaystyle\|g(t)\|^{p}_{L^{p}}+(p-1)\int_{\tau}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|g|^{p}\diver\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}=\|g(\tau)\|^{p}_{L^{p}}+p\int_{\tau}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|g|^{p-2}gf\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}.

This implies that
\|g(t)\|^{p}_{L^{p}}
is absolutely continuous on
[0,T]
and thus differentiable for
a.e.

t\in(0,T)
.
Finally, differentiating the above with respect to
t
leads to (
A.19
).
∎

Finally, consider the following equations for the Lamé operator
L
:

L\boldsymbol{f}=-\alpha\Delta\boldsymbol{f}-\alpha\nabla\diver\boldsymbol{f}=\boldsymbol{g}\qquad\text{in $\mathbb{R}^{n}$}.

(A.21)

We focus on the regularity theory of the solution
\boldsymbol{f}
satisfying
the asymptotic condition:

\boldsymbol{f}\to\boldsymbol{0}\qquad\text{as $|\boldsymbol{x}|\to\infty$}.

(A.22)

The results are stated as follows:

Lemma A.13

(
[
74
]
)
.

Let
q\in(1,\infty)
, and let
\boldsymbol{f}\in D^{1,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

be a weak solution to (
A.21
) with (
A.22
).
Then, if
\boldsymbol{g}\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on
(n,\alpha,k,q)
and independent of
(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{g})
, such that

\|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{g}\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

Appendix B
Conversion of Sobolev Spaces for Spherically Symmetric Functions

This appendix is devoted to showing the conversion of some Sobolev spaces between the M-D Eulerian coordinate
\boldsymbol{x}
and the spherical coordinate
r
for spherically symmetric functions.
Let
n
be the spatial dimensions and
m=n-1
.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
q\in[1,\infty]
,
0\leq a<b\leq\infty
,
\Omega:=\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\,a\leq|\boldsymbol{x}|<b\}
, and
r\in J:=[a,b)
with
r=|\boldsymbol{x}|
.
Then

\mathrm{(i)}

for spherically symmetric function
f\in W^{3,q}(\Omega)
with
f(\boldsymbol{x})=f(r)
,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\sim\|r^{\frac{m}{q}}f\|_{L^{q}(J)},\qquad\qquad\ \,\,\|\nabla f\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\sim\|r^{\frac{m}{q}}f_{r}\|_{L^{q}(J)},

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\sim\Big\|r^{\frac{m}{q}}\big(f_{rr},\frac{f_{r}}{r}\big)\Big\|_{L^{q}(J)},\quad\|\nabla^{3}f\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\sim\Big\|r^{\frac{m}{q}}\Big(f_{rrr},\big(\frac{f_{r}}{r}\big)_{r}\Big)\Big\|_{L^{q}(J)};

\mathrm{(ii)}

for spherically symmetric vector function
\boldsymbol{f}\in W^{4,q}(\Omega)
with
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}f(r)
,

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\sim\|r^{\frac{m}{q}}f\|_{L^{q}(J)},\quad\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\sim\Big\|r^{\frac{m}{q}}\big(f_{r},\frac{f}{r}\big)\Big\|_{L^{q}(J)},

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\sim\Big\|r^{\frac{m}{q}}\Big(f_{rr},\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big)\Big\|_{L^{q}(J)},

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\sim\Big\|r^{\frac{m}{q}}\Big(f_{rrr},\frac{f_{rr}}{r},\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{rr},\frac{1}{r}\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big)\Big\|_{L^{q}(J)},

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{4}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\sim\Big\|r^{\frac{m}{q}}\Big(f_{rrrr},\big(\frac{f_{rr}}{r}\big)_{r},\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{rrr},\Big(\frac{1}{r}(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big)_{r}\Big)\Big\|_{L^{q}(J)}.

Here,
E\sim F
denotes
C^{-1}E\leq F\leq CE
for some constant
C\geq 1

depending only on
n
,
and we have used the following notation for any function space
X
and functions
(h,g_{1},\cdots\!,g_{k})
,

\|h(g_{1},\cdots\!,g_{k})\|_{X}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|hg_{i}\|_{X}.

Proof.

It suffices to prove (ii), since
\nabla f=f_{r}\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}
can be regarded as a vector function
\boldsymbol{h}=h\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}
with
h=f_{r}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{f}=(f_{1},\cdots\!,f_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(r)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}
, and
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\cdots\!,x_{n})^{\top}\in\Omega
. First, it follows from direct calculations that

\displaystyle(f_{k})_{x_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{x_{i}x_{k}}{r^{2}}f_{r}+\frac{\delta_{ik}r^{2}-x_{i}x_{k}}{r^{3}}f,

\displaystyle(f_{k})_{x_{i}x_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}}{r^{3}}f_{rr}+\Big(\frac{\delta_{ij}x_{k}+\delta_{ik}x_{j}+\delta_{jk}x_{i}}{r}-\frac{3x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}}{r^{3}}\Big)\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r},

\displaystyle(f_{k})_{x_{i}x_{j}x_{\ell}}

\displaystyle=\frac{x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{\ell}}{r^{4}}f_{rrr}

\displaystyle\quad+\Big(\frac{\delta_{i\ell}x_{j}x_{k}+\delta_{j\ell}x_{i}x_{k}+\delta_{k\ell}x_{i}x_{j}}{r^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\ \ +\frac{\delta_{ij}x_{k}x_{\ell}+\delta_{ik}x_{j}x_{\ell}+\delta_{jk}x_{i}x_{\ell}}{r^{2}}-\frac{6x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{\ell}}{r^{4}}\Big)\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{rr}

\displaystyle\quad+\Big(\delta_{ij}\delta_{k\ell}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{j\ell}+\delta_{jk}\delta_{i\ell}-\frac{\delta_{i\ell}x_{j}x_{k}+\delta_{j\ell}x_{i}x_{k}+\delta_{k\ell}x_{i}x_{j}}{r^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\ \ -\frac{\delta_{ij}x_{k}x_{\ell}+\delta_{ik}x_{j}x_{\ell}+\delta_{jk}x_{i}x_{\ell}}{r^{2}}+\frac{3x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{\ell}}{r^{4}}\Big)\Big(\frac{1}{r}\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big),

\displaystyle(f_{k})_{x_{i}x_{j}x_{\ell}x_{p}}

\displaystyle=\frac{x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{\ell}x_{p}}{r^{5}}f_{rrrr}

\displaystyle\quad+\Big(\frac{\delta_{ip}x_{j}x_{k}x_{\ell}+\delta_{jp}x_{i}x_{k}x_{\ell}+\delta_{kp}x_{i}x_{j}x_{\ell}+\delta_{\ell p}x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}}{r^{3}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\ \ +\frac{\delta_{i\ell}x_{j}x_{k}x_{p}+\delta_{j\ell}x_{i}x_{k}x_{p}+\delta_{k\ell}x_{i}x_{j}x_{p}}{r^{3}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\ \ +\frac{\delta_{ij}x_{k}x_{\ell}x_{p}+\delta_{ik}x_{j}x_{\ell}x_{p}+\delta_{jk}x_{i}x_{\ell}x_{p}}{r^{3}}-\frac{10x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{\ell}x_{p}}{r^{5}}\Big)\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{rrr}

\displaystyle\quad+\Big(\frac{\delta_{i\ell}\delta_{jp}x_{k}+\delta_{i\ell}\delta_{kp}x_{j}+\delta_{j\ell}\delta_{ip}x_{k}+\delta_{j\ell}\delta_{kp}x_{i}+\delta_{k\ell}\delta_{ip}x_{j}+\delta_{k\ell}\delta_{jp}x_{i}}{r}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\ \ +\frac{\delta_{ij}\delta_{kp}x_{\ell}+\delta_{ij}\delta_{\ell p}x_{k}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jp}x_{\ell}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{\ell p}x_{j}+\delta_{jk}\delta_{ip}x_{\ell}+\delta_{jk}\delta_{\ell p}x_{i}}{r}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\ \ +\frac{\delta_{ij}\delta_{k\ell}x_{p}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{j\ell}x_{p}+\delta_{jk}\delta_{i\ell}x_{p}}{r}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\ \ -\frac{3(\delta_{ip}x_{j}x_{k}x_{\ell}+\delta_{jp}x_{i}x_{k}x_{\ell}+\delta_{kp}x_{i}x_{j}x_{\ell}+\delta_{\ell p}x_{i}x_{j}x_{k})}{r^{3}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\ \ -\frac{3(\delta_{i\ell}x_{j}x_{k}x_{p}+\delta_{j\ell}x_{i}x_{k}x_{p}+\delta_{k\ell}x_{i}x_{j}x_{p})}{r^{3}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\ \ -\frac{3(\delta_{ij}x_{k}x_{\ell}x_{p}+\delta_{ik}x_{j}x_{\ell}x_{p}+\delta_{jk}x_{i}x_{\ell}x_{p})}{r^{3}}+\frac{15x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{\ell}x_{p}}{r^{5}}\Big)\Big(\frac{1}{r}\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big)_{r}.

Then the above expressions yield

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{f}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=k}^{n}|f_{k}|^{2}=|f|^{2},\quad|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}|^{2}=\sum_{i,k=1}^{n}|(f_{k})_{x_{i}}|^{2}=|f_{r}|^{2}+m\Big|\frac{f}{r}\Big|^{2},

\displaystyle|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{f}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}|(f_{k})_{x_{i}x_{j}}|^{2}=|f_{rr}|^{2}+3m\Big|\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big|^{2},

\displaystyle|\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{f}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,k,\ell=1}^{n}\big|(f_{k})_{x_{i}x_{j}x_{\ell}}\big|^{2}=|f_{rrr}|^{2}+6m\Big|\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{rr}\Big|^{2}+(3m^{2}+6m)\Big|\frac{1}{r}\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big|^{2},

\displaystyle|\nabla^{4}\boldsymbol{f}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\!\sum_{i,j,k,\ell,p=1}^{n}\!\big|(f_{k})_{x_{i}x_{j}x_{\ell}x_{p}}\big|^{2}=|f_{rrrr}|^{2}\!+10m\Big|\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{rrr}\Big|^{2}\!+(15m^{2}\!+\!30m)\Big|\Big(\frac{1}{r}\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big)_{r}\Big|^{2},

which imply that

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{f}|\sim|f|,\quad|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}|\sim|f_{r}|+\Big|\frac{f}{r}\Big|,\quad|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{f}|\sim|f_{rr}|+\Big|\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big|,

(B.1)

\displaystyle|\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{f}|\sim|f_{rrr}|+\Big|\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{rr}\Big|+\Big|\frac{1}{r}\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big|,

\displaystyle|\nabla^{4}\boldsymbol{f}|\sim|f_{rrrr}|+\Big|\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{rrr}\Big|+\Big|\Big(\frac{1}{r}\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big)_{r}\Big|.

In addition, since

\frac{f_{rr}}{r}=\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{rr}+\frac{2}{r}\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}
,
\eqref{BB}_{4}
–
\eqref{BB}_{5}
can be also written as

\displaystyle|\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{f}|\sim|f_{rrr}|+\Big|\frac{f_{rr}}{r}\Big|+\Big|\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{rr}\Big|+\Big|\frac{1}{r}\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big|,

(B.2)

\displaystyle|\nabla^{4}\boldsymbol{f}|\sim|f_{rrrr}|+\Big|\big(\frac{f_{rr}}{r}\big)_{r}\Big|+\Big|\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{rrr}\Big|+\Big|\Big(\frac{1}{r}\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big)_{r}\Big|.

Finally, for any spherically symmetric function
g(\boldsymbol{x})=g(r)
, the following spherical coordinate transformation in
\Omega
holds:

\int_{\Omega}g(\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=\omega_{n}\int_{J}g(r)r^{m}\,\mathrm{d}r,

where
\omega_{n}
denotes the surface area of the
n
-sphere.
Therefore, this, together with (
B.1
)–(
B.2
), yields the desired conclusions.
∎

Appendix C
Sobolev Embeddings for Spherically Symmetric Functions

In this appendix, we give the improved Sobolev embeddings for spherically symmetric functions in M-D.

First, we study the Sobolev embeddings of the type:
D^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for spherically symmetric vector functions when
p\in[1,n)
. The following auxiliary lemma is used in our analysis, which can be found in Lemma II.6.1 on

[
28
, page 81]
.

Lemma C.1

(
[
28
]
)
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
n\geq 2
, and let
f\in D^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a scalar or vector function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then
f\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, that is,
\nabla^{j}f\in L^{p}(K)

(j=0,1)
for any bounded domain
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Then we have the Sobolev embeddings of the type:
D^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for spherically symmetric vector functions when
p\in[1,n)
.

Lemma C.2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(r)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}
be any spherically symmetric vector function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}

(n\geq 2)
. If
\boldsymbol{f}\in D^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some
p\in[1,n)
, then
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{\frac{np}{n-p}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and there exists a constant
C(n,p)>0
depending only on
(n,p)
such that

\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\frac{np}{n-p}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(n,p)\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

(C.1)

Proof.

Let
\zeta=\zeta(\boldsymbol{x})\in C^{\infty}_{\rm c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a spherically symmetric cut-off function such that

\zeta=\begin{cases}1&\text{for }|\boldsymbol{x}|\leq 1,\\
\text{smooth}&\text{otherwise},\\
0&\text{for }|\boldsymbol{x}|\geq 2,\end{cases}\qquad\zeta\in[0,1],\qquad|\nabla\zeta|\leq C\ \ \text{for some }C>0,

and let
\zeta_{R}=\zeta_{R}(\boldsymbol{x})=\zeta(\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{R})
for
R>0
.

First, since
\boldsymbol{f}\in D^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, it follows from Lemma
C.1
that
\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f}\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for each
R>0
, which, along with Lemma
A.1
, yields
\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{\frac{np}{n-p}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Consequently, it follows from (
A.1
) in Lemma
A.1
(i) that

\|\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\frac{np}{n-p}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(n,p)\|\nabla(\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f})\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

(C.2)

for some constant
C(n,p)>0
depending only on
(n,p)
. Now, based on (
C.2
) and Lemma
B.1
, we can further derive that

\displaystyle\|\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\frac{np}{n-p}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(n,p)}{R}\Big(\int_{R\leq|\boldsymbol{x}|\leq 2R}|\boldsymbol{f}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}+C(n,p)\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(n,p)}{R}\Big(\int_{R}^{2R}r^{n-1}|f|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}+C(n,p)\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p)\Big(\int_{R}^{2R}r^{n-1-p}|f|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}+C(n,p)\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,p)\big(\big|r^{\frac{n-1}{p}-1}f\big|_{p}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\big)\leq C(n,p)\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

Note that the above inequality holds uniformly for
R>0
, and
\zeta_{R}|\boldsymbol{f}|^{\frac{np}{n-p}}\to|\boldsymbol{f}|^{\frac{np}{n-p}}
for
a.e.

\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
as
R\to\infty
. Thus, it follow from Lemma
A.5
that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\frac{np}{n-p}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\liminf_{R\to\infty}\|\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\frac{np}{n-p}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(n,p)\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

∎

Remark C.1
.

In contrast to (
A.1
)
(
taking
k=1
)
in
Lemma
A.1

\mathrm{(i)}
,
inequality (
C.1
) holds for all spherically symmetric vector functions

\boldsymbol{f}
with
\boldsymbol{f}
merely in
D^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some
p\in[1,n)

and without requiring
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{\frac{np}{n-p}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
which do not holds for general vector functions
\boldsymbol{f}
or scalar functions
f

(
even spherically symmetric ones
)
belonging to
D^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Such examples inlcude

\boldsymbol{f}=(1,\cdots\!,1)^{\top}
or
f=1
.
This distinction lies in the spherical symmetry assumption, since any spherically symmetric constant vector field must vanish. To some extent, the spherical symmetry assumption on
\boldsymbol{f}
serves as a substitute for
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{\frac{np}{n-p}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in
Lemma
A.1

\mathrm{(i)}
when
k=1
and
p<n
.

Remark C.2
.

When
p=n
, the embeddings of the type
:

D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
q\in[1,\infty)
fail to hold for scalar functions or vector functions, even for spherically symmetric ones.
As a counterexample, consider the vector function
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(r)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}
with
f(r)\in C^{\infty}([0,\infty))
,
f(r)=0
if
r\in[0,1]
and
f(r)=\frac{1}{\log r}
if
r\in[2,\infty)
. One may verify that both
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})
and the scalar function
f(\boldsymbol{x})=f(|\boldsymbol{x}|)
belong to
D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, yet neither lies in
L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for any
q\in[1,\infty)
.

Next, we study the Sobolev embeddings of the type:

W^{k+1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(
k\in\mathbb{N}
) for spherically symmetric vector functions. For this, we first need to prove the following auxiliary lemma, which indicates that any spherically symmetric vector function in
W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
can be approximated by a sequence of smooth, spherically symmetric vector functions.

Lemma C.3
.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{f}\in W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

(k\in\mathbb{N}^{*},n\geq 2,\text{ and }p\in(1,\infty))
is a spherically symmetric vector function. Then there exists a sequence of spherically symmetric vector functions
\{\boldsymbol{f}^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}\subset C_{\rm c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\|\boldsymbol{f}^{\epsilon}-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Proof.

Let
\{\omega_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{x})\}_{\varepsilon>0}
be the standard spherically symmetric mollifier defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Then we can show that
\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x}):=(\boldsymbol{f}*\omega_{\epsilon})(\boldsymbol{x})\varphi(\epsilon\boldsymbol{x})
satisfies all the requirements given
in Lemma
C.3
, where
\varphi\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a spherically symmetric function such that
\varphi\geq 0
, and
\varphi=1
on
|\boldsymbol{x}|\leq 1
and
\varphi=0
on
|\boldsymbol{x}|\geq 2
. Here, since the proof of convergence is rather classical, we only check that
\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x})
is spherically symmetric for brevity, which is equivalent to showing that
\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{x})=(\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon})(\boldsymbol{x})
for any matrix
\mathcal{O}\in\mathrm{SO}(n)
. In fact, we have

\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{x})=\varphi(\epsilon\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{x})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\boldsymbol{f}(\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y})\omega_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{y})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{y}.

Changing the coordinates
\boldsymbol{y}=\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{z}
, along with
|\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{x}|=|\boldsymbol{x}|
and
\det\mathcal{O}=1
, gives

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=\varphi(\epsilon\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{x})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\boldsymbol{f}(\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{x}-\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{z})\omega_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{z})(\det\mathcal{O})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=\varphi(\epsilon\boldsymbol{x})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\boldsymbol{f}(\mathcal{O}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z}))\omega_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{z})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=\varphi(\epsilon\boldsymbol{x})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{f})(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})\omega_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{z})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathcal{O}\big(\varphi(\epsilon\,\cdot)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\boldsymbol{f}(\cdot-\boldsymbol{z})\omega_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{z})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{z}\big)\Big)(\boldsymbol{x})=(\mathcal{O}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon})(\boldsymbol{x}).

This completes the proof.
∎

We now have the following result, which shows that the Sobolev embedding:
D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
holds for all spherically symmetric vector functions.

Lemma C.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(r)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}
be any spherically symmetric vector function defined in
\mathbb{R}^{n}

(n\geq 2)
. If
\boldsymbol{f}\in D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
\boldsymbol{f}\in C(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, and there exists a uniform constant
C(n)>0
depending only on
n
such that

\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(n)\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

(C.3)

Proof.

We divide the proof into two steps.

1. Proof for
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})\in W^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
We first consider the spherically symmetric
vector function
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(
n\geq 2
), with
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(r)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}
. Certainly,
f\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}([0,\infty))
. Then it follows from Lemma
A.4
that,
for any
r_{0}\in[0,\infty)
,

|f(r_{0})|^{n}=-\int_{r_{0}}^{\infty}\big(|f|^{n}\big)_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\implies|f|_{\infty}^{n}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|\big(|f|^{n}\big)_{r}\big|\,\mathrm{d}r\leq n\big||f|^{n-1}|f_{r}|\big|_{1},

(C.4)

which, along with Lemma
B.1
and the Hölder inequality, gives that

\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{n}=|f|_{\infty}^{n}\leq n\big||f|^{n-1}|f_{r}|\big|_{1}\leq n\big|r^{-\frac{1}{n}}f\big|_{n}^{n-1}\big|r^{\frac{n-1}{n}}f_{r}\big|_{n}\leq C(n)\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{n}.

(C.5)

Next, for
\boldsymbol{f}\in W^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, thanks to Lemma
C.3
,
there exists a sequence of spherically symmetric vector functions
\{\boldsymbol{f}^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}\subset C^{\infty}_{\rm c}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
satisfying
\|\boldsymbol{f}^{\epsilon}-\boldsymbol{f}\|_{W^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Thus, it follows from (
C.5
) that

\|\boldsymbol{f}^{\epsilon_{1}}-\boldsymbol{f}^{\epsilon_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(n)\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}^{\epsilon_{1}}-\nabla\boldsymbol{f}^{\epsilon_{2}}\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\to 0\qquad\text{as $(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})\to(0,0)$},

which implies that
\boldsymbol{f}^{\epsilon}
converges to some limit
\boldsymbol{g}
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Since
\boldsymbol{f}^{\epsilon}\in C^{\infty}_{\rm c}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
,
\boldsymbol{g}
is bounded and uniformly continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and the uniqueness of the limit yields
\boldsymbol{f}=\boldsymbol{g}\in C(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
. Therefore, it follows from (
C.5
) that (
C.3
) holds for
\boldsymbol{f}\in W^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
i.e.
,

\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\|\boldsymbol{f}^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(n)\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=C(n)\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

2. Proof for
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})\in D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Let
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})\in D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and let
(\zeta,\zeta_{R})
be the spherically symmetric cut-off functions defined in Lemma
C.2
. First, using Lemma
C.1
and
\boldsymbol{f}\in D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
yields that

\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f}\in W^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for each
R>0
.

Next, we show that

\lim_{R\to\infty}\big\|\nabla(\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f})-\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\big\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=0.

(C.6)

It follows from Lemma
B.1
and the absolute continuity of the integral
that, as
R\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla(\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f})-\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\big\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\nabla(\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f})-\zeta_{R}\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\big\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\big\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}-\zeta_{R}\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\big\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq\big\||\nabla\zeta_{R}||\boldsymbol{f}|\big\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\big\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}-\zeta_{R}\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\big\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{R}\Big(\int_{R\leq|\boldsymbol{x}|\leq 2R}|\boldsymbol{f}|^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\Big)^{\frac{1}{n}}+\big\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}-\zeta_{R}\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\big\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(n)}{R}\Big(\int_{R}^{2R}r^{n-1}|f|^{n}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big)^{\frac{1}{n}}+\big\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}-\zeta_{R}\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\big\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C(n)\Big(\int_{R}^{2R}r^{n-1}\Big|\frac{f}{r}\Big|^{n}\,\mathrm{d}r\Big)^{\frac{1}{n}}+\big\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}-\zeta_{R}\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\big\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C(n)\Big(\int_{R\leq|\boldsymbol{x}|\leq 2R}|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}|^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\Big)^{\frac{1}{n}}+\Big(\int_{|\boldsymbol{x}|\geq R}|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}|^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\Big)^{\frac{1}{n}}\to 0,

which yields claim (
C.6
). Clearly, we can also obtain from (
C.6
)
that there exists a sufficiently large
R_{0}>0
such that

\|\nabla(\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f})\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq 2\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\qquad\text{for all $R\geq R_{0}$}.

(C.7)

Therefore, it follows from
\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f}\in W^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, (
C.7
),
and Step 1 above that
\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f}\in C(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
for each
R>0
and

\|\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(n)\|\nabla(\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f})\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(n)\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\qquad\text{for all $R\geq R_{0}$}.

(C.8)

Combining (
C.6
) and (
C.8
) yields that, for
R_{1},R_{2}\geq R_{0}
, when
(R_{1},R_{2})\to(\infty,\infty)
,

\|\zeta_{R_{1}}\boldsymbol{f}-\zeta_{R_{2}}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(n)\|\nabla(\zeta_{R_{1}}\boldsymbol{f})-\nabla(\zeta_{R_{2}}\boldsymbol{f})\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\to 0,

which implies that
\{\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f}\}_{R\geq R_{0}}\subset C(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, converging to
\boldsymbol{f}\in C(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
as
R\to\infty
. Finally, we obtain from (
C.8
) that (
C.3
) holds
for
\boldsymbol{f}\in D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
:

\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\lim_{R\to\infty}\|\zeta_{R}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(n)\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

The proof of Lemma
C.4
is completed.
∎

The following lemma is on the Sobolev embedding of the type:
W^{3,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Lemma C.5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(r)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}
be any spherically symmetric vector function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}

(n\geq 2)
. If
\boldsymbol{f}\in W^{3,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
\boldsymbol{f}\in C^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, and there exists a uniform constant
C(n)>0
depending only on
n
such that

\|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(n)\|\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

(C.9)

Proof.

We give the proof of (
C.9
) for
\boldsymbol{f}\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
since the rest of Lemma
C.5
can be derived by using an argument similar
to Step 1 in the proof of Lemma
C.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{f}\in C^{\infty}_{\rm c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a spherically symmetric vector function with form:
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(r)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}
.
Clearly, it follows from Lemma
B.1
that
\big(f_{rr},(\frac{f}{r})_{r}\big)\in C_{\rm c}^{1}([0,\infty))
. Thus, following the same calculations (
C.4
)–(
C.5
) in Step 1 of
the proof of Lemma
C.4
,
with
f
replaced by
f_{rr}
and
(\frac{f}{r})_{r}
, respectively, we obtain

\displaystyle|f_{rr}|_{\infty}^{n}

\displaystyle\leq n\big||f_{rr}|^{n-1}|f_{rrr}|\big|_{1}\leq n\big|r^{-\frac{1}{n}}f_{rr}\big|_{n}^{n-1}\big|r^{\frac{n-1}{n}}f_{rrr}\big|_{n}\leq C(n)\|\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{n},

\displaystyle\Big|\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big|_{\infty}^{n}

\displaystyle\leq n\Big|\big|\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\big|^{n-1}\big|\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{rr}\big|\Big|_{1}\leq n\Big|r^{-\frac{1}{n}}\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big|_{n}^{n-1}\Big|r^{\frac{n-1}{n}}\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{rr}\Big|_{n}\leq C(n)\|\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{n},

which, together with Lemma
B.1
, gives that, for all
\boldsymbol{f}\in C^{\infty}_{\rm c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(n)\|\nabla^{3}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

∎

Remark C.3
.

Since the embeddings
:

W^{k+1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

(k=0,2)

do not hold in the general case,
Lemmas
C.4
–
C.5
amount
to an extension of the classical Sobolev embedding theorem for the spherically symmetric vector functions. However,
Lemmas
C.4
–
C.5
cannot be extended to scalar functions or
general vector functions, and the embedding
W^{2,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
cannot hold even for spherically symmetric vector functions. We will provide several counterexamples in
Remarks
C.4
–
C.5
.

Based on Lemmas
C.4
–
C.5
, we can obtain the Sobolev embeddings for scalar functions.

Lemma C.6
.

Let
f(\boldsymbol{x})
be any spherically symmetric function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}

(n\geq 2)
. If
f\in W^{k+2,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

(k=0,2)
, then
f\in C^{k+1}(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, and there exists a uniform constant
C(k,n)>0
depending only on
(k,n)
such that

\|\nabla^{k+1}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(k,n)\|\nabla^{k+2}f\|_{L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

(C.10)

Proof.

Note that, for any spherically symmetric function
f
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\nabla f=f_{r}\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}
can be regarded as a new vector function
\boldsymbol{h}=h\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}
with
h=f_{r}
.

Let
f\in W^{k+2,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

(k=0,2)
. Then
\boldsymbol{h}\in W^{k+1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which, along with Lemmas
C.4
–
C.5
, leads to
\nabla^{k}\boldsymbol{h}\in C(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, that is,
\nabla^{k+1}f\in C(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
∎

We first give a counterexample to indicate that the embedding:
W^{2,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
cannot hold for spherically symmetric vector functions.

Remark C.4
.

It is important to note that the embedding
W^{2,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
does not hold for spherically symmetric vector functions.
For example, let
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(r)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}
be a spherically symmetric vector function with

f(r)\in C^{\infty}[0,\infty),\qquad f(r)=r|\log r|^{\nu}\text{ on $[0,e^{-1})$},\qquad f(r)=0\text{ on $[1,\infty)$}.

(C.11)

We aim to show that
\boldsymbol{f}\in W^{2,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\boldsymbol{f}\notin W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
\nu\in(0,\frac{n-1}{n})
. Clearly, since
\boldsymbol{f}
is compactly supported, it suffices to consider the integrability and boundedness of
\boldsymbol{f}
and its derivatives on
B_{*}=\{\boldsymbol{x}:\,|\boldsymbol{x}|<e^{-1}\}
. First, a direct calculation gives that, for
r\in[0,e^{-1})
,

\displaystyle f_{r}

\displaystyle=|\log r|^{\nu}-\nu|\log r|^{\nu-1},\quad\frac{f}{r}=|\log r|^{\nu},

(C.12)

\displaystyle f_{rr}

\displaystyle=-\nu\frac{|\log r|^{\nu-1}}{r}+\nu(\nu-1)\frac{|\log r|^{\nu-2}}{r},\quad\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}=-\nu\frac{|\log r|^{\nu-1}}{r}.

Then, since
\nu>0
, we can directly obtain from
Lemma B.1
that

\|\nabla\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{*})}\geq C^{-1}\Big\|\big(f_{r},\frac{f}{r}\big)\Big\|_{L^{\infty}(0,e^{-1})}=\infty\implies\boldsymbol{f}\notin W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(C.13)

Next, we show that
\boldsymbol{f}\in W^{2,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. By (
C.11
)
–
(
C.12
) and
Lemma
B.1
, it suffices to prove that
\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{n}(B_{*})
. Indeed, since
\nu\in(0,\frac{n-1}{n})
, it follows from
Lemma
B.1
and the coordinate transformation
z=|\log r|
that

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{n}(B_{*})}^{n}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big\|r^{\frac{n-1}{n}}\Big(f_{rr},\big(\frac{f}{r}\big)_{r}\Big)\Big\|_{L^{n}(0,e^{-1})}^{n}\leq C\int_{0}^{e^{-1}}\Big(\frac{|\log r|^{n\nu-n}}{r}+\frac{|\log r|^{n\nu-2n}}{r}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=C\int_{1}^{\infty}(z^{n\nu-n}+z^{n\nu-2n})\,\mathrm{d}z<\infty.

This implies
\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{n}(B_{*})
. Therefore, we construct a spherically symmetric vector function
\boldsymbol{f}\in W^{2,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\boldsymbol{f}\notin W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

As indicated by
Lemma
B.1
, for a general spherically symmetric function
\boldsymbol{f}
, the
L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
-boundedness of
\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{f}
does not imply any decay behavior of
\nabla\boldsymbol{f}
at
\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{0}
. This suggests that
\nabla\boldsymbol{f}
may behave singular at
\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{0}

(\text{see \eqref{cl03}})
, even when
\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Next, we give a counterexample to Lemma
C.4
for spherically symmetric scalar functions and general vector functions.

Remark C.5
.

It is important to note that the embedding
:

D^{1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow C(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
fails to hold for spherically symmetric scalar functions or general vector functions. For example, consider the function
f=f(z)
defined on
[0,\infty)
satisfying

f(z)=\begin{cases}0&\,\,\mathrm{on}\ \{z=0\},\\[4.0pt]
\big|\log z\big|^{\frac{1}{3}}&\,\,\mathrm{on}\ (0,e],\\[4.0pt]
e^{2}z^{-2}&\,\,\mathrm{on}\ (e,\infty).\end{cases}

(C.14)

Define
g(\boldsymbol{x})\!=\!f(|\boldsymbol{x}|)
and
\boldsymbol{h}(\boldsymbol{x})
with
h_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(|x_{i}|)

(1\leq i\leq n)
.
It can be checked that
(g,\boldsymbol{h})
admits the weak derivatives
(\partial_{j}g,\partial_{j}\boldsymbol{h})\in L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

(1\leq j\leq n)
, while
(g,\boldsymbol{h})\notin L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

As revealed by
Lemma
B.1
, these differences arise because the
L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
-integrability for the gradient of spherically symmetric vector functions provides additional constraints near the symmetry center. Specifically, let
\boldsymbol{\tilde{f}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\tilde{f}(r)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}
. Then it follows from
Lemma
B.1
that

\displaystyle\nabla\boldsymbol{\tilde{f}}\in L^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

\displaystyle\iff\underline{r^{-\frac{1}{n}}\tilde{f}\in L^{n}(I)}_{(\star)}\quad\,\,\text{and}\quad\,\,r^{\frac{n-1}{n}}\tilde{f}_{r}\in L^{n}(I).

Remarkably, to some extent,
(\star)
above implicitly governs the decay behavior of
\boldsymbol{\tilde{f}}
near the symmetry center since
r^{-\frac{1}{n}}\notin L^{n}(0,\epsilon)
for any
\epsilon>0
, thereby preventing the possible concentration of
\boldsymbol{\tilde{f}}
at the origin, such as the logarithmic growth of
f
as
z\to 0
in (
C.14
).

Finally, we give some further interpretations to Lemmas
C.4
–
C.6
.

Remark C.6
.

In fact, we can show that, for any smooth spherically symmetric vector function, its “even-order derivatives” must vanish at
\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{0}
. Specifically, let
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(r)\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{r}
be any smooth spherically symmetric vector function. Since
f_{i}(-\boldsymbol{x})=-f_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})

(i=1,\cdots\!,n)
,
\partial^{\varsigma}f_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})
are odd functions for any even
|\varsigma|\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\partial^{\varsigma}f_{i}(-\boldsymbol{x})=-\partial^{\varsigma}f_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\implies\partial^{\varsigma}f_{i}(\boldsymbol{0})=0\qquad\,\,\text{for all even $|\varsigma|\in\mathbb{N}$}.

The above discussion indicates that the “even-order derivatives” of a spherically symmetric vector function satisfy an additional constraint at
\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{0}
.
Thus, it is natural to conjecture that the Sobolev embeddings of the type:
W^{k+1,n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for spherically symmetric vector functions, shown in
Lemmas
C.4
–
C.5
, hold for arbitrary even number
k=2l

(l\in\mathbb{N})
. However, a detailed proof lies beyond the scope of this paper. We leave the rigorous justification of this conjecture as an open problem to interested readers.
